</p></boxed-text>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0007"><label>2</label><title>BACKGROUND</title><p>Pyoderma (bacterial skin infection) is common in dogs and is among the top four presentations that lead to antimicrobial prescribing.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0001" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>1</sup>
</xref> Given the urgent need to reduce inappropriate use of antimicrobials and limit the spread of antimicrobial resistance among human and animal pathogens, the skin offers clear opportunities for antimicrobial stewardship owing to its accessibility for diagnosis and topical antimicrobial treatment. These guidelines aim to provide clinicians with practical treatment recommendations for managing canine pyoderma, while at the same time promoting rational use of antimicrobials. Another objective was to revise outdated recommendations regarding treatment duration, choice and dosing of antimicrobial agents.</p><sec id="vde13342-sec-0008"><title>What is included?</title><p>The guidelines deal with canine bacterial skin infections, primarily involving <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus pseudintermedius</italic> and other staphylococci, both meticillin‐susceptible and meticillin‐resistant. For information on wounds or abscesses and less common skin infections involving organisms such as <italic toggle="yes">Mycobacterium</italic> spp., <italic toggle="yes">Mycoplasma</italic> spp., <italic toggle="yes">Nocardia</italic> spp. or <italic toggle="yes">Dermatophilus congolensis</italic>, readers are referred to other sources.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0002" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>2</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0003" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>3</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0004" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>4</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>The document includes an update of the superficial bacterial folliculitis guidelines previously published on behalf of the International Society for Companion Animal Infectious Diseases (ISCAID)<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref> based on recent advances in the field. It also adds new recommendations for the diagnosis and antimicrobial therapy of surface and deep pyoderma in dogs and provides a brief overview specific to meticillin‐resistant staphylococci (MRS), expanding on previous MRS guidelines.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref> Based on recent microbiological and immunological insights, the term ‘surface pyoderma’ has become controversial and may be more appropriately replaced with ‘dysbiosis’ (see Section ‘Clinical presentations’). However, the clinical presentations known as surface pyodermas remain included here in order to highlight the best treatment options.</p></sec><sec sec-type="methods" id="vde13342-sec-0009"><title>Methods</title><p>A major challenge for companion animal antimicrobial guidelines is the paucity of objective data, particularly from adequately designed and implemented randomised controlled trials.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0007" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>7</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0008" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>8</sup>
</xref> As with the previous guidelines,<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref> and those pertaining to other diseases,<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0009" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>9</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0010" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>10</sup>
</xref> recommendations are based on data from published studies of varying strengths combined with general principles of infectious disease, antibacterial agent pharmacology, and consensus from an author panel with international clinical expertise in veterinary dermatology, internal medicine, microbiology and pharmacology.</p><p>Recommendations were developed using an iterative process during online meetings. Each author contributed to different parts of the document, yet all reviewed the entire document and voted on all recommendations. Recommendations that did not receive 100% approval were discussed and revised further; final consensus for a recommendation was unanimous from all authors unless stated otherwise.</p><p>Systematic literature searches were conducted for clinical treatment studies of dogs with pyoderma; publications meeting the search criteria are listed in the Tables <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0001" ref-type="supplementary-material">[Link]</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0004" ref-type="supplementary-material">[Link]</xref> in the Supporting information. Conference abstracts that were not available as peer‐reviewed full publications were excluded. The full methods and search strategy, including search terms, were deposited for public access within the SYREAF (Systematic reviews for animals and food) site.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0011" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>11</sup>
</xref> Databases were searched on 22 April 2022 and updated on 23 December 2023.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0012" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>12</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>The quality of evidence from published therapeutic trials was included in our treatment recommendations where appropriate using the Strength of Recommendation Taxonomy (SORT)<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0013" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>13</sup>
</xref> as a system utilised in veterinary publications relating to skin disease (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0001" ref-type="table">1</xref>).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0014" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>14</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0015" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>15</sup>
</xref>
</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0001" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 1</label><caption><p>Descriptors for level of evidence and strength of recommendation categories used based on the Strength of Recommendation Taxonomy (SORT) (modified from Ebell et al.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0013" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>13</sup>
</xref>).</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" colspan="2" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Level of evidence (LoE)</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Definition for treatment studies</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Good quality, patient orientated</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">‘High‐quality’ randomised controlled trial (RCT) <italic toggle="yes">or</italic> meta‐analysis of consistent RCTs with ≥10 dogs per group</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">2</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Limited quality patient‐orientated</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">‘Low‐quality’ RCT downgraded owing either to &lt;10 dogs per group, lack of separate assessment of groups, lack of specific clinical interpretation <italic toggle="yes">or</italic> prospective case series (cohort study) containing ≥10 dogs</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">3</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Other evidence</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Prospective case series containing &lt;10 dogs <italic toggle="yes">or</italic> a retrospective case series (any size)</td></tr></tbody></table><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" colspan="2" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Strength of recommendation (SOR)</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Definition</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">A</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Strong</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Based on good‐quality patient‐orientated evidence (one or more LoE 1 studies)</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">B</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Moderate</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Based on limited‐quality patient‐orientated evidence (one or more LoE 2 studies)</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">C</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Weak</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Based on consensus combined with disease‐orientated evidence or case series (LoE 3)</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap><p>Following this internal process, 15 veterinarians, including general practitioners and specialists from relevant disciplines, from different continents, with an interest in skin disease and antimicrobial stewardship, were invited to critique the draft document. Their comments were evaluated and any modifications to recommendations were voted upon using a Delphi‐style consensus before submission for publication.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0010"><title>How to use the guidelines</title><p>The guidelines should be interpreted as general recommendations appropriate for good clinical management in the majority of cases. They should be used as a basis for case‐specific decision‐making by the attending veterinarian, taking into account the dog's general health status and overall circumstances. Furthermore, this document is intended for an international readership. It remains the responsibility of any veterinarian prescribing antimicrobials to be familiar with their regional antimicrobial susceptibility trends, availability of antimicrobial drugs and national prescribing regulations applicable to antimicrobials. Additionally, the guidelines can be used by national veterinary organisations to develop or update national guidelines.</p><p>Given that bacterial skin infection always occurs secondary to a primary underlying cause, it is incumbent upon attending veterinarians to identify and address the primary aetiology to achieve resolution of an infection and avoid recurrence; this may require consideration of multiple recommendations beyond those for antimicrobial treatment.</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0011"><label>3</label><title>TYPES OF CANINE PYODERMA AND BACTERIAL PATHOGENS</title><p>Bacterial skin infections present in many different clinical forms, and a varied, inconsistent terminology for the different presentations has evolved. Within these guidelines, we have adopted the classification by histological depth of infection, which distinguishes surface, superficial and deep pyoderma.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0016" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>16</sup>
</xref> In contrast to a problem‐based classification of pyoderma, which has been proposed to help in diagnosis,<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0017" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>17</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0018" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>18</sup>
</xref> the classification by depth is most helpful in choosing the most appropriate treatment approach (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0001" ref-type="fig">1</xref>).</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0001" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 1</label><caption><p>Histopathological classification of canine pyoderma by depth, and recommended treatment approaches (topical antimicrobial therapy including antiseptics and topical antibiotics).</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-3" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g002.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g002.jpg?><?image-size 49993?><?image-md5 f0b045bd90d92cf6c4f1a4aeebe07b38?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 550?><?image-original-width 1064?><?image-scaled-height 366?><?image-scaled-width 709?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/f0b045bd90d9/VDE-36-234-g002.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g002.gif?><?thumb-size 4404?><?thumb-md5 96bff9b0dbcf684a2db9eb99f09863ea?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 80?><?thumb-scaled-width 154?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/96bff9b0dbcf/VDE-36-234-g002.gif?></graphic></fig><sec id="vde13342-sec-0012"><title>Differentiating depth of infection</title><p>Surface, superficial and deep pyoderma are differentiated through a combination of lesion types and location on the body. No single lesion is pathognomonic for a specific depth of pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0002" ref-type="table">2</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0002" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 2</label><caption><p>Commonly recognised presentations of canine pyoderma by depth of infection as used in these guidelines and examples of typical lesions and findings (more details given in Tables <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0005" ref-type="table">5</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0007" ref-type="table">7</xref> and <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0009" ref-type="table">9</xref>).</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Depth/type of pyoderma</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Clinical presentations</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Most common lesions and frequent clinical findings</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Surface</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0001"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0075"><p>Pyotraumatic acute moist dermatitis (‘hot spot’)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0076"><p>Intertrigo (skin‐fold dermatitis)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0077"><p>Bacterial overgrowth syndrome (see Section ‘Clinical presentations’ for controversy over inclusion as an infection)</p></list-item></list>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0002"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0078"><p>Erythema, exudation, alopecia, pruritus</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0079"><p>Erythema, malodour</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0080"><p>Erythema, surface exudate</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Superficial</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0003"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0081"><p>Superficial bacterial folliculitis (SBF)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0082"><p>Exfoliative superficial (spreading) pyoderma</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0083"><p>Impetigo</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0084"><p>Mucocutaneous pyoderma</p></list-item></list>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0004"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0085"><p>Papules, follicular pustules, epidermal collarettes, crusts</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0086"><p>Epidermal collarettes, scales, crust</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0087"><p>Papules, interfollicular pustules, epidermal collarettes</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0088"><p>Erythema, crusting, hypopigmentation</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Deep</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0005"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0089"><p>Widespread furunculosis/cellulitis</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0090"><p>Localised furunculosis/cellulitis, for example:
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0006"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0091"><p>Pyotraumatic folliculitis and furunculosis</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0092"><p>Acral lick dermatitis</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0093"><p>Infected interdigital nodules</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0094"><p>Callus pyoderma</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0095"><p>Chin pyoderma/chin ‘acne’</p></list-item></list>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0096"><p>Postgrooming furunculosis</p></list-item></list>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Can be present in any presentation of deep pyoderma:
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0007"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0097"><p>Haemorrhagic crusts, haemopurulent discharge, fistulae (sinuses), ulcers, nodules, plaque, ill‐defined swelling</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0098"><p>Pain, lymphadenomegaly</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap><p>Differentiation based on clinical signs may be challenging in some cases,<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0019" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>19</sup>
</xref> especially because not all pyoderma types have been clearly defined yet. For example, pyotraumatic dermatitis (surface pyoderma) can closely resemble pyotraumatic folliculitis/furunculosis (deep pyoderma) and controversy remains whether these are distinct diseases or a continuum with different severity.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0020" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>20</sup>
</xref> Likewise, the lesions associated with lip‐fold intertrigo (surface pyoderma), mucocutaneous pyoderma (superficial pyoderma) and mucocutaneous lupus erythematosus (sterile immune‐mediated disease) can closely resemble each other or overlap.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0021" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>21</sup>
</xref> Until a better understanding of these conditions is available, treatment decisions need to be made on a case‐by‐case basis and within the wider clinical context. A stepwise approach, which addresses the infectious component with topical antimicrobial therapy first, followed by re‐assessment, will be prudent and effective in many cases. Skin biopsy for histopathological evaluation may need to be considered if lesions remain and if other differential diagnoses need to be investigated.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0013"><title>Bacterial pathogens typically involved in canine pyoderma</title><p>Staphylococci are the predominant bacterial pathogens in canine pyoderma. According to a retrospective study from Canada, staphylococci accounted for 76.8% of bacterial isolates from canine skin samples between 1994 and 2013, followed by streptococci (8.7%), <italic toggle="yes">Pseudomonas aeruginosa</italic> (4.4%) and <italic toggle="yes">Escherichia coli</italic> (4.2%).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0022" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>22</sup>
</xref> Recent studies based on 16S ribosomal RNA gene sequencing have confirmed that staphylococci are the most abundant bacteria in swabs collected from skin lesions in atopic dogs.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0023" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>23</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Among the staphylococci, the coagulase‐positive <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> is found in &gt;90% of all types of canine pyoderma, with even higher percentages from superficial pyoderma based on data from clinical studies and from laboratory submissions, irrespective of its meticillin‐susceptibility status.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0024" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>24</sup>
</xref> Less information is available for surface pyoderma, where typically a more mixed microbial population, including other Gram‐positive bacteria (e.g. other staphylococci, enterococci, streptococci), Gram‐negative bacteria (e.g. <italic toggle="yes">Pseudomonas</italic> spp.) and yeasts (e.g. <italic toggle="yes">Malassezia pachydermatis</italic>) can be expected.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0025" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>25</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0026" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>26</sup>
</xref> For deep pyoderma, our literature search in 2022 identified <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> as the main pathogen, with streptococci, Gram‐negative bacteria, and anaerobes found in approximately 40% of cases, highlighting the importance of bacterial culture and susceptibility testing in deep infections (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0010" ref-type="table">10</xref>).</p><p>Other coagulase‐positive staphylococci such as <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> (formerly <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> subsp. <italic toggle="yes">coagulans</italic>) can colonise and infect dogs, yet typically account for &lt;10% of laboratory submissions from canine pyoderma.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0027" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>27</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0028" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>28</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0029" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>29</sup>
</xref>
<italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> was originally divided into two subspecies based on their coagulase status: <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> subsp. <italic toggle="yes">schleiferi</italic> as the coagulase‐negative variant and <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> subsp. <italic toggle="yes">coagulans</italic> as the coagulase‐positive variant.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0030" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>30</sup>
</xref> They gained relevance owing to high rates of meticillin resistance among clinical isolates.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0031" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>31</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0032" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>32</sup>
</xref> The subspecies were previously shown to be genotypically indistinct by commonly employed diagnostic methods, and not to differ in biological behaviour or pathogenic potential.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0031" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>31</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0033" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>33</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0034" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>34</sup>
</xref> Reclassification of <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> subspecies as separate species—the coagulase‐negative <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> and the coagulase‐positive <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans—</italic>has been recommended based on genomic analysis.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0034" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>34</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0035" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>35</sup>
</xref> Studies have suggested that <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> is more commonly isolated from humans, while <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> is more commonly isolated from dogs.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0034" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>34</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0036" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>36</sup>
</xref> Additional studies utilising larger numbers of isolates from wider geographical regions are necessary to confirm this proposed host affinity. Given the difficulty in distinguishing between the subspecies using common methods, and the lack of uniform adoption of the new species classification by diagnostic laboratories, this guideline document will employ <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> to refer to both species. Both <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic> should be considered as potential pathogens when obtained from a properly collected skin culture sample.</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0014"><label>4</label><title>DIAGNOSTIC APPROACH AND TESTS</title><p>Routinely adopting a three‐step approach to every case of suspected pyoderma before prescribing antimicrobial treatment can reduce inappropriate use of antimicrobials, support a speedier successful treatment outcome and help to avoid relapses (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0002" ref-type="fig">2</xref>).</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0002" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 2</label><caption><p>Stepwise diagnostic approach to a dog with pyoderma.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-5" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g009.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g009.jpg?><?image-size 223618?><?image-md5 b1073490239f3c2a2dd6c9d74089faec?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1759?><?image-original-width 938?><?image-scaled-height 1172?><?image-scaled-width 625?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/b1073490239f/VDE-36-234-g009.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g009.gif?><?thumb-size 8353?><?thumb-md5 b2eead7da410555ea867b2620dcb4497?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 188?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/b2eead7da410/VDE-36-234-g009.gif?></graphic></fig><sec id="vde13342-sec-0015"><title>Skin examination</title><p>The skin is uniquely accessible for inspection and sampling. It should be examined in its entirety to determine the extent and distribution of skin lesions. To accurately assess lesion types, long hair may need to be trimmed in affected areas; scissors are often preferred over clippers to avoid damage to lesions before sampling.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0016"><title>Cytology</title><p>Cytology from affected skin should be performed in every case of suspected canine pyoderma to confirm a bacterial cause.</p><p>Cytological evaluation of skin lesions is a key tool in practicing responsible antimicrobial stewardship and one of the most valuable tests in the diagnosis, management and follow‐up of dogs with skin diseases. The diagnostic sensitivity of cytology results has been assessed as 93% in canine superficial pyoderma, based on the presence of intracellular (phagocytosed) cocci and neutrophils.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0037" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>37</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Many skin lesions associated with pyoderma also occur with noninfectious disease, and it is therefore essential to confirm a bacterial cause before choosing antibacterial therapy. For example, pustules—the hallmark lesions for canine SBF—are also seen as the early lesions in pemphigus foliaceus, which is a sterile immune‐mediated disease. Likewise, papules and erythema are recognised as common lesions in canine atopic dermatitis.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0038" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>38</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Microscopic examination of skin samples for cells (cytology) can be readily achieved in‐house. A rapid staining kit and a microscope with a bright light source, usually a × 100 objective lens, and ×10 ocular lens for ×1000 magnification of host cells, bacteria and fungi will be needed, similar to that required to examine blood smears.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0039" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>39</sup>
</xref> Cytology should be part of every skin examination and, luckily, is achievable in any practice set‐up, with very little time or funds required, and yields high‐value information (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0001" ref-type="boxed-text">1</xref>).</p><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0001" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 1</label><caption><title>Benefits of skin cytology</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0017"><label>1</label><p>Can be performed in‐house with immediate results.</p><p>Rapid staining methods such as a modified Wright–Giemsa stain (e.g. Diff‐Quik) are adequate; they do not require Gram stains or other complicated stains.</p><p>Can confirm the diagnosis of pyoderma and the need for topical or systemic antimicrobial therapy (typically finding cocci and/or rods within neutrophils).</p><p>Can differentiate pyoderma from diseases with similar clinical signs yet different aetiologies (e.g. <italic toggle="yes">Malassezia</italic> dermatitis, sterile granulomatous skin diseases, pemphigus foliaceus).</p><p>Is needed to interpret bacterial culture results by confirming that relevant organisms have been cultured, particularly when multiple pathogens are reported.</p><p>Can be used to monitor response to treatment and confirm resolution of infection.</p></sec></boxed-text><p>Cytology samples are first assessed qualitatively for the presence of bacteria, their morphologies (cocci, rods), and for the presence of inflammatory cells. Samples from staphylococcal pyoderma lesions will typically show intracellular cocci (within neutrophils or macrophages, with or without some keratinocytes (squames or corneocytes; Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0003" ref-type="fig">3</xref>)). Secondly, assessing the abundance of bacteria may point towards their relevance. Both semiquantitative and quantitative methods for evaluation of skin cytology have been described.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0037" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>37</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0040" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>40</sup>
</xref> Individual bacteria in low numbers may be identified in samples from healthy skin depending on sample site. Thresholds for numbers of bacteria required to define infection have not been validated. Our group agreed that the following findings are supportive of pyoderma:
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0008"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0099"><p>intracellular bacteria <italic toggle="yes">or</italic>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0100"><p>extracellular bacteria with nuclear streaking (also called streaming) <italic toggle="yes">or</italic>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0101"><p>as in the case of bacterial overgrowth (see Section ‘Surface pyoderma’), large numbers of bacteria from lesional skin in the absence of inflammatory cells</p></list-item></list>
</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0003" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 3</label><caption><p>Examples of typical cytology findings from canine pyoderma. Samples taken from skin lesions and stained with modified Wright‐Giemsa stain (e.g. Diff‐Quick). ×1000. (a) Cocci, most likely staphylococci, some dividing into pairs and quadruplets, in and among neutrophils; impression smear of pus expressed from a pustule. (b) Large numbers of cocci without neutrophils from a dog with bacterial overgrowth syndrome; acetate tape sample from skin on the abdomen. (c) Large numbers of rods (bacilli) among degenerate neutrophils (some with nuclear streaming or streaking); impression smear of pus from a moist lesion on the neck (image courtesy S. Shaw). (d) Mixed microbial morphologies (rods and cocci); impression smear from a dog's lip fold.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-7" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g010.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g010.jpg?><?image-size 165506?><?image-md5 4c33d5d94f73c957918e6f3f8f9106b4?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 953?><?image-original-width 1064?><?image-scaled-height 635?><?image-scaled-width 709?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/4c33d5d94f73/VDE-36-234-g010.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g010.gif?><?thumb-size 8084?><?thumb-md5 b7446bf47d291359d81b6a76b6434eaa?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 90?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/b7446bf47d29/VDE-36-234-g010.gif?></graphic></fig><p>However, with a sensitivity of 93% for superficial pyoderma, the absence of cocci does not entirely exclude a diagnosis. In cases of deep pyoderma, the absence or presence of bacteria from surface samples has little relevance for diagnosis. Recognition of other morphologies (rods [bacilli]; <italic toggle="yes">Malassezia</italic> yeast) or of mixed populations is important for treatment decisions (e.g. in determining the need for concurrent antifungal therapy). Estimating the predominant bacterial morphologies is also crucial to guide meaningful interpretation of bacterial culture and antimicrobial susceptibility testing (BC/AST) results (see Section ‘Bacterial culture and antimicrobial susceptibility testing (BC/AST)’).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0018"><title>Investigations into underlying primary causes</title><p>Pyoderma is always secondary to underlying primary causes, and these must be considered at the first occurrence.</p><p>All dogs are thought to be colonised by staphylococci, but most do not develop pyoderma. Infection arises when the skin barrier is altered by predisposing factors. Although the pathogenesis of pyoderma is not fully understood, microbiological and molecular studies have shown that <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> isolates involved in skin infections are indistinguishable and likely originate from commensal <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> populations.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0041" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>41</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0042" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>42</sup>
</xref> A primary underlying cause is always required to trigger or facilitate bacterial skin disease, even if such a cause is not immediately apparent. The concept of ‘idiopathic’ or ‘primary’ pyoderma is no longer tenable.</p><p>Trauma (e.g. abrasions, cuts, friction and chronic pressure) is probably the most common underlying primary cause that leads to bacterial skin infection, yet ectoparasitic infestation and atopic skin disease are likely to be the most troublesome in small animal practice, accounting for 80% of cases in one retrospective study.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0043" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>43</sup>
</xref> Additionally, altered cornification and/or systemic diseases such as endocrinopathies and neoplasia can lead to structural, immunological or chemical changes in the skin and, consequently, secondary pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0003" ref-type="table">3</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0003" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 3</label><caption><p>Typical underlying causes and commonly associated types of canine pyoderma.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Disease group</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Disease</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Most common type of pyoderma</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" rowspan="3" valign="top" colspan="1">Allergic skin disease</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Atopic dermatitis</td><td align="left" rowspan="3" valign="top" colspan="1">
<p>SBF, ExP</p>
<p>Pyotraumatic dermatitis particularly frequent with flea bite hypersensitivity</p>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Adverse food reaction</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Flea bite hypersensitivity</td></tr><tr><td align="left" rowspan="3" valign="top" colspan="1">Ectoparasitic diseases</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Sarcoptic mange</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, rarely localised DP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Demodicosis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">DP, SBF</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Cheyletiellosis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, ExP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">Infectious diseases</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Dermatophytosis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, DP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Leishmaniosis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">DP, SBF</td></tr><tr><td align="left" rowspan="3" valign="top" colspan="1">Endocrine disorders</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Hypothyroidism</td><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">SBF, ExP, rarely DP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Hyperadrenocorticism</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Sex hormone imbalances</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, ExP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Autoimmune diseases</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pemphigus foliaceus</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, ExP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Follicular dysplasias</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Colour dilution alopecia</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, ExP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Keratinisation/ cornification disorders</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ichthyoses</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, ExP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">Others</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Sebaceous adenitis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SBF, ExP</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Acne</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">DP</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0001"><fn id="vde13342-note-0001"><p>Abbreviations: DP, deep pyoderma; ExP, exfoliative superficial pyoderma; SBF, superficial bacterial folliculitis.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>A comprehensive overview of the diagnosis and treatment of underlying primary causes for pyoderma is beyond the scope of these guidelines, but key information can be obtained from thorough history‐taking and a comprehensive physical examination. A minimum diagnostic dataset for all dogs with pyoderma should include microscopic examination of coat brushings (for evidence of fleas or <italic toggle="yes">Cheyletiella</italic> spp.), hair plucks (trichogram) for evidence of <italic toggle="yes">Demodex</italic> spp., dermatophyte hyphae and/or fungal spores and/or skin scrapings to investigate the presence of <italic toggle="yes">Sarcoptes scabiei</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">Demodex</italic> spp. and the confirmation of ongoing appropriate acaricide and insecticide prophylactic treatment. Additional tests may include those for dermatophytosis (e.g. Wood's lamp examination, trichogram, fungal culture and PCR)<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0044" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>44</sup>
</xref> and histopathological evaluation of skin biopsies for immune‐mediated and neoplastic conditions. In particular, nodular skin lesions resulting from bacterial infection need to be differentiated from infected granulomas resulting from fungi or protozoa, sterile granulomatous disease (e.g. panniculitis), neoplasia and foreign body reactions by biopsy, special stains, macerated tissue culture and, in some cases, molecular techniques.</p><p>Blood sampling for haematological, serum biochemical and, depending on results, endocrine testing and urinalysis may be indicated if an underlying cause remains elusive. Nonthyroidal illness syndrome (formerly euthyroid sick syndrome) can be associated with skin infection and can complicate the interpretation of laboratory results. Pairing T4/free T4 testing with TSH measurement is recommended to support accurate diagnosis.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0019"><title>Bacterial culture and antimicrobial susceptibility testing (<styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">BC</styled-content>/<styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">AST</styled-content>)</title><p>BC/AST is recommended to guide drug choices whenever systemic therapy is planned and is preferred over empirical choices.</p><p>BC/AST is always strongly recommended when there is an increased risk of resistance to common empirically chosen antimicrobials, for example, if there is a history of recent or frequent antimicrobial use, previous isolation of meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus pseudintermedius</italic> (MRSP), <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic> (MRSA) or <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> (MRSC) (formerly <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi</italic>) or in regions or clinics with a high prevalence of meticillin‐resistance.</p><p>BC/AST should always be paired with cytology to guide correct interpretation of laboratory results.</p><p>BC/AST is never contraindicated, but its cost is often a financial barrier. In the interest of antimicrobial stewardship and patient care, laboratories and practices should be encouraged to price these valuable tests reasonably to improve uptake, prevent treatment failure and limit inappropriate antimicrobial use.</p><p>BC/AST is not needed to make or exclude a diagnosis of pyoderma. Samples taken even from healthy skin often yield bacteria, including <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic>, as part of the normal colonising microbiota in the majority of dogs (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0002" ref-type="boxed-text">2</xref>).</p><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0002" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 2</label><caption><title>When is BC/AST needed in a dog with surface, superficial or deep pyoderma?</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0020"><label>1</label><p>Surface pyoderma
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0009"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0102"><p>Not needed (see Section ‘Surface pyoderma’)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0103"><p>Treatment‐of‐choice is always topical antimicrobial therapy alone.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0104"><p>BC/AST results are not meaningful for topical agents owing to the lack of breakpoints for topical therapy.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0105"><p>Cytology will provide the required information to select active ingredients.</p></list-item></list>
</p><p>Superficial pyoderma
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0010"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0106"><p>Not needed if treated with topical antimicrobial therapy alone.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0107"><p>Always strongly recommended when there is concern about drug resistance:
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0011"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0108"><p>History of recurrent pyoderma</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0109"><p>More than one course of systemic antimicrobials within the past 6 months (for any condition, not just skin disease)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0110"><p>History of MRS infection in the affected dog or their household</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0111"><p>No improvement of lesions after 5–7 days of systemic antimicrobial therapy at an appropriate dose and with good compliance.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0112"><p>Emergence of new lesions during therapy</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0113"><p>High regional prevalence of MRSP (refer to local surveillance data or contact local laboratories)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0114"><p>Rod bacteria and/or polymicrobial findings on cytology</p></list-item></list>
</p></list-item></list>
</p><p>Deep pyoderma
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0012"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0115"><p>Always strongly recommended (see Section ‘Deep pyoderma’)</p></list-item></list>
</p></sec></boxed-text><p>Although ‘trial therapy’ with systemic antimicrobials chosen empirically is frequently prescribed, often effective and generally safe, BC/AST‐based prescribing remains preferable to reduce the risk of treatment failure and inappropriate antimicrobial use whenever systemic therapy is needed. Our recommendation to perform BC/AST is ‘strong’ for cases with well‐recognised risk factors for MRSP (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0002" ref-type="boxed-text">2</xref>). Important risk factors are previous MRSP infection, repeated courses of systemic antimicrobials, frequent visits to veterinary clinics or having previously been seen at referral hospitals. MRSP prevalences have been reported higher from referral centres.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0045" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>45</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0046" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>46</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>In addition to guiding drug choices for systemic therapy, laboratory testing is needed to identify multidrug‐resistant (MDR) pathogens such as MRSP, MRSA and MRSC (meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic>) so that practice infection control measures can be implemented appropriately (see Section ‘Meticillin‐resistant staphylococcal pyoderma’).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref> Failure to recognise an infection caused by MDR bacteria can result in inappropriate antimicrobial use, a protracted clinical course and associated patient discomfort, difficulty in assessing the need for other clinical interventions required for addressing the underlying primary disease, increased client dissatisfaction and expense and a prolonged risk of transmission.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>The majority of MRS can be accurately identified by diagnostic laboratories based on phenotypical susceptibility testing while molecular confirmation of important marker genes (e.g. <italic toggle="yes">mecA</italic>) may be required for research purposes.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref> By contrast, molecular antibiograms that have recently been marketed in some countries for clinical purposes need to be interpreted carefully within the clinical and microbiological context to avoid overcalling drug‐resistance.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0047" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>47</sup>
</xref>
</p><sec id="vde13342-sec-0021"><title>How to interpret <styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">BC</styled-content>/<styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">AST</styled-content> reports from dogs with pyoderma?</title><p>Samples should be submitted to laboratories that follow recognised standards for BC/AST and use breakpoints for pathogens isolated from veterinary species, currently only published by the Clinical and Laboratory Standards Institute (CLSI).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0048" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>48</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>With the emergence of multidrug resistance among veterinary staphylococci, accurate bacterial species identification has become critical. This is, first, because the epidemiology and owner advice vary between MRSP, MRSA and MRSC, and, secondly, because laboratory clinical breakpoints are different for <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic>. Differentiation between <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> is challenging based on phenotypic and biochemical tests alone.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0049" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>49</sup>
</xref> To date, the most accurate and rapid method for bacterial identification is by matrix‐assisted laser desorption/ionisation‐time of flight (MALDI‐TOF) mass spectrometry. MALDI‐TOF has been validated for <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> identification and is now considered the method of choice in veterinary microbiology worldwide.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0050" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>50</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Antimicrobial susceptibility tests need to be performed according to approved standards to allow relevant conclusions to be gained from the results. Small laboratories or in‐clinic laboratories may not have the infrastructure and microbiological expertise required to accurately perform and interpret these diagnostic tests, which can lead to inappropriate treatment choices with negative consequences for patient care.</p><p>Where multiple bacterial isolates are reported, treatment should be prescribed to target the predominant pathogen—in most cases <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic>. Cytology will allow identification of the most abundant morphologies so that unnecessary use of broad‐spectrum antimicrobials to cover the reported composite susceptibility profile of multiple isolates can be avoided (see Section ‘Culture‐based treatment’).</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0022"><title>How to take representative skin samples</title><p>The best sampling technique will vary between lesion type (moist versus dry), expected depth of infection and purpose of sample collection (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0004" ref-type="table">4</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0004" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 4</label><caption><p>Tips for sampling skin lesions for cytological evaluation and bacterial culture.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Lesion type</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comment</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">For cytological evaluation</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">For bacterial culture</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pustule (or bulla)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pustule content optimal owing to low risk of contamination with commensals</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Lance pustule with sterile needle, transfer content directly onto glass slide (impression smear)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">No surface disinfection. Lance with sterile needle and transfer pus (or haemopurulent material) to sterile swab</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pus (exudative lesions)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">From skin surface or expressed from draining tracts/sinuses. Remove dried surface material before sampling</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Impression smear directly onto slide or use swab to sample, then roll onto slide</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Squeeze and discard surface pus using a single wipe with 70% alcohol. Allow alcohol to dry, then squeeze again and sample using a sterile swab</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Crust, epidermal collarette with peripheral crusting</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Underside of crust or skin below is expected to yield representative bacteria</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Lift crust using sterile needle, sample exposed skin with swab, slide or tape</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Lift crust using sterile forceps or needle. Sample the exposed exudate or skin or beneath the ‘leading edge’ of an epidermal collarette with a sterile swab</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Papule</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Papule content is preferrable to surrounding skin</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pinch skin to express fluid or blood or tape strip to lift material surrounding the papule</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pinch the skin to express fluid or blood onto a sterile swab; surface sampling may be unrewarding</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Dry lesions (old epidermal collarettes)</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Border of epidermal collarettes preferred over centre of lesion</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Tape strip to lift diagnostic material or roll/rub swab two to three times over lesion, then smear on slide</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">No surface disinfection. Roll sterile swab beneath the leading edge/across the border of the collarette two to three times</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Erythematous skin</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">When no other lesions are found or when surface pyoderma is suspected (e.g. intertrigo)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Tape strip sampling or rotate swab several times over affected skin (or within a fold), then roll onto slide</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Rarely needed</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Deep lesions</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">For example, interdigital nodules or plaques</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Needle aspirates or tissue impression after biopsy</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Provide sedation, local or general anaesthesia as appropriate. Clip hair, clean and disinfect surface (wipe with 70% alcohol, let dry before sampling). Consider wearing gloves. Obtain tissue by biopsy (or fine needle aspirate) and submit in a sterile container or bacterial transport medium.</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap><p>In surface pyoderma, most sampling techniques should provide a good yield of diagnostic material.</p><p>For superficial pyoderma, it has been shown that sampling intact pustules or skin beneath crusts or the margins of epidermal collarettes is best.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0051" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>51</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0052" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>52</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0053" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>53</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0054" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>54</sup>
</xref> One study showed that multiple pustules from the same dog with SBF always harboured the same strain of <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> (as determined by pulsed‐field gel electrophoresis), while epidermal collarettes and crusts harboured multiple genotypically distinct strains of <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> with divergent antimicrobial susceptibility profiles, supporting that intact pustules should be preferentially targeted for sampling.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0055" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>55</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>In cases of deep pyoderma, exudative lesions (fresh pus from ulcerated skin surface, material draining from sinuses or fistulae, pustule content) are most suitable for sampling, provided that the most superficial, less representative material can be removed before sampling (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0004" ref-type="table">4</xref>). If the nidus of infection is located below the skin surface (e.g. some interdigital nodules and acral lick dermatitis lesions), a tissue biopsy is best obtained under aseptic conditions with the surface part of the skin removed so that infected tissue is examined or submitted for BC/AST (tissue culture after maceration or mincing). Where this is not possible, fine needle aspiration may be chosen. Invasive sampling will require appropriate analgesia or anaesthesia (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0004" ref-type="table">4</xref>). Surface swabs in deep pyoderma will often miss relevant pathogens, as shown in a study of dogs with acral lick dermatitis where surface culture only predicted deep tissue isolates in 8 of 22 cases.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0056" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>56</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Acetate tape preparations and glass slide impression smears have a fair‐to‐good agreement for detection of bacteria, yet impression smears may be superior for detection of neutrophils.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0057" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>57</sup>
</xref> Swabs and aspirates are submitted in transport medium (typically provided with the swab) for BC/AST. Cotton swabs are suitable, yet flocked swabs, coated in short nylon fibres, placed in the provided liquid‐based collection tubes, may improve bacterial yield.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0058" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>58</sup>
</xref> When sampling draining or nodular lesions, where involvement of nonstaphylococcal, potentially zoonotic pathogens is possible, gloves should be worn and extended culture for anaerobic or fastidious bacteria or fungi should be requested.</p><p>The impact of antimicrobial therapy on bacterial culture results remains unknown yet it seems intuitive that if cocci are seen on cytology, a sample submitted for BC/AST should yield bacterial growth regardless of current treatment. However, if practical, pausing treatment for ≥2 days before sampling for culture may be prudent, even though there is no scientific evidence to underpin this recommendation.</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0023"><label>5</label><title>SURFACE PYODERMA</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0024"><title>Clinical presentations</title><p>Surface pyoderma encompasses three common, clinically distinct presentations where bacterial or mixed microbial overgrowth is confined to the upper layers of the epidermis. <bold>Pyotraumatic dermatitis</bold> (‘acute moist dermatitis, hot spot’) is characterised by a rapid onset, often within hours. It presents with an exudative, localised, pruritic or often painful lesion considered to be caused by self‐trauma promoted by pruritus, discomfort or pain affecting the area.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0020" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>20</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0059" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>59</sup>
</xref> In <bold>intertrigo</bold> (fold dermatitis), moisture and skin secretions trapped in folds are likely to facilitate microbial dysbiosis of commensal bacteria and yeast.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0026" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>26</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0060" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>60</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0061" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>61</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0062" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>62</sup>
</xref> Little is known about <bold>bacterial overgrowth syndrome</bold> (BOG) beyond its nonspecific skin lesions such as erythema, hyperpigmentation and lichenification<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0018" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>18</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0063" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>63</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0064" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>64</sup>
</xref> and its unusual cytological findings; large numbers of bacteria are typically seen as evidence for overgrowth, but neutrophils and macrophages are lacking or almost absent (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0005" ref-type="table">5</xref>; Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0004" ref-type="fig">4</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0005" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 5</label><caption><p>Typical presentations and findings of surface pyoderma in dogs.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Presentation</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Typical lesions &amp; distribution</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Differential diagnoses</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Potential inciting causes</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Cytological and histopathological findings</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pyotraumatic dermatitis (<italic toggle="yes">acute</italic> moist dermatitis, ‘hot spot’)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Localised area of erythema, exudate, swelling, matted hair</p>
<p>Can rapidly progress to erosion, ulceration</p>
<p>Cheek, neck, lateral thigh, rump</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pyotraumatic folliculitis and/or furunculosis (deep pyoderma)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Atopic dermatitis, other hypersensitivities, ectoparasites</p>
<p>Ear disease, anal sac, disease, arthritis</p>
<p>Subcutaneous injection site reactions, adverse topical drug reaction</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Predominantly cocci, extracellular and within degenerative neutrophils</p>
<p>Superficial neutrophilic and/or eosinophilic perivascular inflammation and dermal oedema, necrosis and erosion to ulceration, crusting<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0020" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>20</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0067" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>67</sup>
</xref>
</p>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Intertrigo (skin‐fold dermatitis)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Erythema, swelling, alopecia, crusting, exudate, erosion, malodour, hyper‐ (rarely de‐)pigmentation</p>
<p>Any skin folds, typically facial, lip, vulvar, tail folds</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Demodicosis, mucocutaneous pyoderma (superficial pyoderma), DLE/MCLE</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Friction from skin rubbing against skin</p>
<p>Breed predispositions (e.g. bulldog, Shar Pei, bloodhound, spaniels) and folds developing with obesity</p>
<p>Atopy, other hypersensitivities</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Predominantly extra‐ and intracellular cocci but often mixed with rods and yeast, inflammatory cells<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0025" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>25</sup>
</xref>
</p>
<p>Epidermal hyperplasia with neutrophilic exocytosis, lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate in superficial dermis (‘lichenoid band’)<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0068" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>68</sup>
</xref>
</p>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Bacterial overgrowth syndrome (BOG)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Erythema, exudate, lichenification, hyperpigmentation</p>
<p>Ventral abdomen, interdigital, pinnae</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<italic toggle="yes">Malassezia</italic> dermatitis or overgrowth, other chronic inflammatory or hyperplastic skin changes</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Atopic dermatitis, other hypersensitivities</p>
<p>Keratinisation disorders</p>
<p>Endocrinopathies</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>High numbers of cocci yet no or few neutrophils</p>
<p>Perivascular‐interstitial hyperplastic and hyperkeratotic dermatitis with lymphocytic exocytosis, focal or diffuse spongiosis and occasionally epidermal neutrophilic micro‐abscesses, pigmentary incontinence</p>
</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0002"><fn id="vde13342-note-0002"><p>Abbreviation: DLE/MCLE, discoid lupus erythematosus/mucocutaneous cutaneous lupus erythematosus.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0004" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 4</label><caption><p>Examples of surface pyoderma in dogs. (a) Pyotraumatic dermatitis (‘hot spot’) on the lateral neck of a Newfoundland. (b) Intertrigo (skin fold dermatitis) affecting the facial folds of a bulldog. (c) Bacterial overgrowth syndrome on the palmar interdigital skin of a Labrador retriever.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-9" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g003.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g003.jpg?><?image-size 73936?><?image-md5 2d8b6bdaa35504ccb750f12950d07a63?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 279?><?image-original-width 1064?><?image-scaled-height 186?><?image-scaled-width 709?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/2d8b6bdaa355/VDE-36-234-g003.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g003.gif?><?thumb-size 11662?><?thumb-md5 a8b6b0e4fd7e14d07036f3171ffff96d?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 52?><?thumb-scaled-width 200?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/a8b6b0e4fd7e/VDE-36-234-g003.gif?></graphic></fig><p>The aetio‐pathogenesis of surface pyodermas is poorly studied yet all are secondary to inflammatory underlying primary conditions, such as atopic skin disease or friction from skin rubbing against adjacent fold skin. The term ‘dysbiosis’ has been proposed for the microbial changes, and overgrowth of <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> with a reduction in microbial diversity has been identified in microbiome studies in atopic dogs.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0065" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>65</sup>
</xref> The inclusion of such dysbioses among the pyodermas has become controversial because bacteria are not invading tissue as in the traditional sense of ‘infection’.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0066" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>66</sup>
</xref> For BOG in particular, the term ‘pyoderma’ is a misnomer as neither pus nor inflammatory cells are detectable on the skin.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0025"><title>Diagnosis</title><p>The diagnosis of surface pyoderma is clinical, based on a compatible history and the presence of suggestive skin lesions in typical locations. Cytology is then needed to confirm bacterial involvement and the requirement for antimicrobial treatment. Cytology will also help with tailoring decisions on active ingredients for topical therapy in some cases (see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’). BC/AST is not needed as only topical treatment should be used. Diagnosis and correction of the underlying primary disease will be key to achieving long‐term treatment success (see Section ‘Diagnostic approach and diagnostic tests’).</p><p>Skin lesions of pyotraumatic dermatitis can resemble those of a form of deep pyoderma known as ‘pyotraumatic folliculitis and furunculosis’ (see Section ‘Deep pyoderma’). Hair around the lesion may need to be clipped and the skin carefully examined for the absence of signs suggestive of deep pyoderma. Papules, pustules, haemorrhagic crusts, swelling and furuncles in the periphery are termed ‘satellite’ lesions, and would indicate haematogenous spread and deep pyoderma requiring a different treatment approach (see Section ‘Deep pyoderma’). A study of 44 dogs showed that lesions of pyotraumatic dermatitis can be difficult to differentiate from pyotraumatic folliculitis/furunculosis clinically and even histopathologically.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0020" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>20</sup>
</xref> A similar uncertainty about depth of infection also may be encountered in intertrigo affecting the lip folds where clinical signs may resemble those of mucocutaneous pyoderma (see Section ‘Superficial pyoderma’).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0026"><title>Treatment recommendations for surface pyoderma</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0027"><title>Topical antimicrobial therapy</title><p>Although commonly seen in practice, few clinical treatment trials have been published for surface pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0006" ref-type="table">6</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0006" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 6</label><caption><p>Clinical trials on treatment of surface pyoderma in dogs (English language publications, full manuscripts).</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1"/><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">References</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Agents tested</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Study design</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">SORT level of evidence</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Dogs (n) enrolled/completed</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Duration</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Outcome</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" rowspan="4" valign="top" colspan="1">Pyotraumatic dermatitis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Cobb et al., 2005<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0025" ref-type="bibr">
