<sup>64</sup>
</xref>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">3% chlorhexidine (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 22) versus 2.5% benzoyl peroxide (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 18) shampoo twice weekly</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">RCT</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">34/40</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">6 weeks</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Reduction in bacterial and lesion scores in both groups and no difference in time to cytological cure between groups</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0003"><fn id="vde13342-note-0003"><p>Abbreviations: BOG, bacterial overgrowth; RCT, randomised, controlled trial.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>Topical antimicrobial therapy is the treatment‐of‐choice for surface pyoderma (SOR A).</p><p>Topical antiseptics (e.g. chlorhexidine) should be prioritised over topically used antibiotics (e.g. fusidic acid, mupirocin) (see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’). Antiseptics are expected to have broad activity against most relevant skin pathogens and active ingredients can therefore be chosen empirically. If topical antibiotics are needed, active ingredients should be chosen based on cytology results (see Section ‘Which active ingredient?’). For localised lesions of pyotraumatic dermatitis, gels or sprays may be most suitable (following cleaning of discharge or crust). Wipes or gels will allow good penetration into deeper parts of folds in intertrigo, and frequent application of product, at least once daily and typically twice daily, will be needed. Shampoos or sprays may be most suited to widespread affected areas (see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’). Analgesia should be considered to facilitate topical therapy, particularly for pyotraumatic dermatitis lesions which are often painful.</p><p>Systemic antimicrobial therapy should be avoided in cases of surface pyoderma given the location and direct accessibility of microbial pathogens. The efficacy of topical therapy alone was equivalent to systemic therapy in dogs with pyotraumatic dermatitis<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0020" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>20</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0025" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>25</sup>
</xref> Topical treatment alone is effective in BOG and intertrigo (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0006" ref-type="table">6</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0028"><title>Anti‐inflammatory therapy</title><p>Combination therapy of topical antimicrobial therapy with topical glucocorticoids or with a short course (5–7 days) of systemic glucocorticoids at anti‐inflammatory doses (SOR A) or antipruritic medication may be helpful in cases of pyotraumatic dermatitis and of intertrigo where an inflammatory or pruritic primary cause is involved.</p><p>A major role for inflammation and hence the need for anti‐inflammatory treatment in surface pyodermas is widely accepted.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0069" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>69</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0070" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>70</sup>
</xref> Of the eight clinical studies published for surface pyoderma, three RCTs showed good efficacy of the glucocorticoid products (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0006" ref-type="table">6</xref>; Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0003" ref-type="boxed-text">3</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0004" ref-type="boxed-text">4</xref>).</p><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0003" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 3</label><caption><title>Example of approach to a dog with pyotraumatic dermatitis (‘hot spot’)</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0029"><label>1</label><p>
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0013"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0116"><p>Approach the lesion carefully; it may be painful. Consider systemic or local pain relief.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0117"><p>Clip, including the surrounding area</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0118"><p>Clean to remove debris and biofilm</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0119"><p>Apply antiseptic (e.g. 2%–4% chlorhexidine) or topical antibiotic with or without topical glucocorticoid preparation once or twice daily for 7 days.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0120"><p>Consider 5–7 days of oral prednisolone (0.5–1.0 mg/kg body weight once daily) or dexamethasone (0.1 mg/kg once daily) or of a Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitor to reduce inflammation, pain and itch.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0121"><p>Investigate and manage underlying primary causes (e.g. review ectoparasite prophylaxis, examine ears and anal sacs and consider underlying allergic disease)</p></list-item></list>
</p></sec></boxed-text><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0004" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 4</label><caption><title>Example of approach to a dog with intertrigo (skin‐fold dermatitis)</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0030"><label>1</label><p>
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0014"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0122"><p>Clean affected skin to remove debris and biofilm</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0123"><p>Apply antiseptic therapy, for example wipe once or twice daily with 2%–4% chlorhexidine wipes or apply as a solution or spray.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0124"><p>If rods dominate on cytology, consider adding an agent with activity against Gram‐negative bacteria, for example silver sulfadiazine or gentamicin‐containing creams or drops.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0125"><p>For facial‐fold dermatitis in proximity to the eyes, formulations with topical antibiotics suitable for ophthalmic use, for example fusidic acid‐ or gentamicin‐containing eye drops, can be considered.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0126"><p>In cases that present with severe erythema, swelling and pain, consider the use of topical formulations combining antimicrobials (antiseptics or topically used antibiotics) with topical glucocorticoids. Apply twice daily for 7–10 days, then re‐examine.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0127"><p>If erosions or ulcers are noticed, the use of hypochlorous acid products (safe in open wounds, eyes and mucous membranes) may be preferred until the defects have healed.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0071" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>71</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0072" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>72</sup>
</xref>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0128"><p>Depending on the case, consider weight loss, dental treatment and management of urinary disease.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0129"><p>In some dogs, surgical management of folds (cheiloplasty, vulvoplasty) or caudectomy for ingrown tails may be needed.</p></list-item></list>
</p></sec></boxed-text><p>Clinical resolution of surface pyoderma can be assumed when pre‐treatment lesions have significantly improved or resolved and cytological results from previously affected areas are normal. This can be expected within 7–14 days. Re‐assessment of clinical signs by a veterinarian and repeat cytology are recommended if progress is deemed poor by the owner. Compliance and the diagnosis of underlying primary causes may need to be reviewed and corrected.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0032"><title>Long‐term management</title><p>Antiseptic treatment can be continued proactively on previously affected skin, potentially life‐long, where the primary underlying causes cannot be resolved (e.g. skin folds) and the risk of recurrence remains (SOR C).</p><p>Data on long‐term management of surface pyodermas are not published, but recurrences are well‐recognised when primary causes remain elusive or cannot easily be corrected. Weight loss or surgical management of skin folds may be helpful in some cases of intertrigo. Frequencies of proactive antiseptic applications will vary and need to be identified by tapering until recurrence is seen on a case‐by‐case basis. Although there have been intermittent reports of in vitro chlorhexidine tolerance in staphylococci, the concentrations of chlorhexidine achieved through appropriate clinical use far exceed the maximum minimum inhibitory concentrations (MICs) described in the literature, despite decades of use of this active ingredient. No true antimicrobial resistance resulting in clinical treatment failure of staphylococcal infection using chlorhexidine has yet been described.</p><p>If diagnostic assessment for underlying causes has raised suspicion for primary allergic causes, proactive therapy with topical anti‐inflammatory therapy may be considered to prevent relapses.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0073" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>73</sup>
</xref> Application of astringent powders, herbal products to heal pyotraumatic dermatitis or barrier creams to reduce friction and maceration in skin folds, and thereby provide a skin environment less prone to microbial dysbiosis, have been mentioned historically yet evidence for efficacy is not available.</p></sec></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0033"><label>6</label><title>SUPERFICIAL PYODERMA</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0034"><title>Clinical presentations</title><p>Superficial pyoderma is defined as a bacterial infection affecting the epidermis and the hair follicle. Four clinical presentations are described (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0007" ref-type="table">7</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0007" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 7</label><caption><p>Typical presentations and characteristics of superficial pyoderma in dogs.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Presentation</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Typical lesions</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Distribution</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Differential diagnoses</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Cytological &amp; histopathological findings</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comments</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Superficial bacterial folliculitis (SBF)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Follicular papules, pustules, epidermal collarettes, multifocal areas of alopecia</p>
<p>In short‐coated breeds, patchy alopecia (‘moth‐eaten’ coat) may predominate</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Ventral abdomen, medial thighs, dorsal neck</p>
<p>Can be generalised</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Other follicular diseases: demodicosis, dermatophytosis, sebaceous adenitis, sterile pustular dermatitis, leishmaniosis</p>
<p>Follicular papules may mimic urticaria in short‐coated breeds</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Intra‐ or extracellular bacterial cocci and neutrophils</p>
<p>Luminal folliculitis and subcorneal pustules within follicular openings containing Gram‐positive bacterial cocci</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Most common type of superficial pyoderma in dogs. Often pruritic</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Exfoliative superficial pyoderma (previously ‘superficial spreading’)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Large expansile epidermal collarettes (in the absence of papules, pustules), central alopecia and hyperpigmentation, erythematous rim</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ventrum, can be generalised</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Dermatophytosis, erythema multiforme, drug eruptions (cutaneous adverse drug reactions), VCLE</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Intra‐ or extracellular bacterial cocci and neutrophils</p>
<p>Intracorneal clefts, which may contain basophilic cellular debris and bacterial cocci</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Shetland sheepdogs and collies predisposed</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Impetigo and bullous impetigo</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Nonfollicular pustules, often fragile and with erythematous rim, yellow or green content, epidermal collarettes.</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Groin, axillae.</p>
<p>Can be generalised</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pemphigus foliaceus (also associated with large, fragile pustules and crusts)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Intra‐ or extracellular bacterial cocci and neutrophils</p>
<p>Neutrophilic subcorneal pustules, often spanning multiple follicular units, and containing Gram‐positive bacterial cocci</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Puppies and juvenile dogs. As ‘bullous impetigo’ in immunocompromised adults</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Mucocutaneous pyoderma</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Erythema, swelling, crusts, exudate, erosions, ulcers, pigmentary changes, peripheral alopecia</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Mucocutaneous junctions, lips, perioral skin and also eyelids, around nose, perianal and peri‐genital</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Intertrigo (lip‐/facial‐fold dermatitis), discoid or mucocutaneous lupus erythematosus (DLE/MCLE)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Neutrophils, nuclear streaming, and bacterial cocci (intra‐ or extracellular)</p>
<p>Histological changes overlap with (DLE/MCLE) Superficial dermal infiltrates of lymphocytes, plasma cells, lichenoid band and basal cell apoptosis</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">German shepherd dogs predisposed</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0004"><fn id="vde13342-note-0004"><p>Abbreviations: DLE/MCLE, discoid lupus erythematosus/mucocutaneous cutaneous lupus erythematosus; VCLE, vesicular cutaneous lupus erythematosus.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>
<bold>Superficial bacterial folliculitis</bold> is by far the most common type of superficial pyoderma in the dog, with <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> isolated most often in pure culture.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref> The dog's predisposition to folliculitis is thought to be facilitated by a missing sebum plug around the follicle opening; it is likely that there are other anatomical and physiological characteristics, too.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0077" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>77</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0078" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>78</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Hallmark lesions of SBF are often found concurrently on the same dog, starting with erythematous follicular papules, progressing to pustules which will rupture to form epidermal collarettes with an erythematous peripheral ring and a healing, sometimes hyperpigmented centre; eventually leaving focal areas of alopecia. Any area of haired skin can be affected, yet predilection sites in medium‐ to long‐coated breeds are the ventral abdomen and medial thighs, while the distribution is often truncal in short‐haired breeds with focal areas of alopecia being most prominent, giving the dogs a ‘moth‐eaten’ appearance.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref> In some cases of SBF, mainly in short‐coated dogs, follicular papules, pustules and crusts also may cause elevation of tufts of hairs, which may be mistaken for urticaria and are persistent (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0005" ref-type="fig">5</xref>). Occasionally, scaling, loss of coat lustre, increased shedding or malodour may be the presenting signs. In some breeds, such as the American cocker spaniel, crusts may be notably hyperkeratotic and plaque‐like. Postinflammatory hyperpigmentation is common and may persist following lesion resolution.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0018" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>18</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0052" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>52</sup>
</xref>
</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0005" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 5</label><caption><p>Examples of superficial pyoderma in dogs. (a) Superficial bacterial folliculitis affected the inguinal region of a Golden retriever. (b) On the trunk and lateral thigh of an American pitbull terrier.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-11" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g004.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g004.jpg?><?image-size 78169?><?image-md5 126638852692774fa0ada85c9d88d221?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 389?><?image-original-width 1064?><?image-scaled-height 259?><?image-scaled-width 709?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/126638852692/VDE-36-234-g004.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g004.gif?><?thumb-size 13687?><?thumb-md5 ab61e569d45375bf306a29e893a818e9?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 73?><?thumb-scaled-width 200?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/ab61e569d453/VDE-36-234-g004.gif?></graphic></fig><p>
<bold>Exfoliative superficial pyoderma</bold> (previously termed ‘superficial spreading pyoderma’) presents with strikingly large, expansile epidermal collarettes, resembling large target lesions (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0006" ref-type="fig">6</xref>). This phenotype of superficial pyoderma is particularly recognised in Shetland sheepdogs, Australian and German shepherd dogs, and collie breeds, although other breeds and mixed breeds can be affected.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0018" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>18</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0019" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>19</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0054" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>54</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0079" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>79</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0080" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>80</sup>
</xref> The histopathological findings of exfoliative superficial pyoderma are distinct from those for SBF owing to the presence of frequent intracorneal clefts which may contain basophilic cellular debris and bacterial cocci.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0054" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>54</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0081" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>81</sup>
</xref> A less common form of exfoliative superficial pyoderma has been described in dogs, characterised by generalised erythema with large sheets of scale.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0019" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>19</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0054" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>54</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0080" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>80</sup>
</xref>
</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0006" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 6</label><caption><p>Exfoliative superficial pyoderma in a Shetland sheepdog.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-13" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g006.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g006.jpg?><?image-size 81278?><?image-md5 d560c7a557d03d333ae3f0aef1a600fb?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 619?><?image-original-width 933?><?image-scaled-height 413?><?image-scaled-width 622?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/d560c7a557d0/VDE-36-234-g006.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g006.gif?><?thumb-size 9706?><?thumb-md5 a25313b528d2b7dfdd436c0870e53fb4?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 80?><?thumb-scaled-width 120?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/a25313b528d2/VDE-36-234-g006.gif?></graphic></fig><p>
<bold>Impetigo</bold> in juvenile dogs is often visually distinctive from SBF owing to the large size of the pustules, their nonfollicular orientation, and a lack of concurrent erythematous papules (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0007" ref-type="fig">7</xref>). Risk factors that have been suggested in young dogs include poor nutrition, endoparasitism, or concurrent viral infection.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0018" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>18</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0019" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>19</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0080" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>80</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0081" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>81</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0082" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>82</sup>
</xref> Bullous impetigo, with similarly large, nonfollicular pustules and also with bullae in glabrous areas, is recognised in immunocompromised adult dogs, particularly in association with iatrogenic or spontaneous hyperadrenocorticism.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0002" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>2</sup>
</xref> It is different from impetigo in humans, which is a highly contagious skin infection caused by exfoliative toxin‐producing staphylococci or group A streptococci.</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0007" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 7</label><caption><p>(a) Impetigo on the ventral abdomen of a Siberian husky puppy. (b) Mucocutaneous pyoderma of the eyelid margin and medial canthus in a soft‐coated Wheaten terrier.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-15" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g005.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g005.jpg?><?image-size 210661?><?image-md5 b7763b7994a09b93f24f5fe3eee32522?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1421?><?image-original-width 921?><?image-scaled-height 947?><?image-scaled-width 614?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/b7763b7994a0/VDE-36-234-g005.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g005.gif?><?thumb-size 14210?><?thumb-md5 45dac7c12591eec81a483807d033b7d2?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 154?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/45dac7c12591/VDE-36-234-g005.gif?></graphic></fig><p>
<bold>Mucocutaneous pyoderma</bold> is a poorly characterised entity, recognised by erythema, erosions, ulcers, crusts and pigmentary changes in mucocutaneous locations that resolve with antimicrobial treatment and extend beyond the epidermis (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0007" ref-type="fig">7</xref>). Microbes involved are not well documented yet are likely to represent skin microflora and <italic toggle="yes">Pseudomonas</italic> spp., particularly in moist areas. When affecting the perioral or perivulvar areas, mucocutaneous pyoderma can be difficult to differentiate from intertrigo (see Section ‘Surface pyoderma’). Further characterisation of these two presentations is required to confirm whether they are distinct presentations or whether mucocutaneous pyoderma is a continuum of a dysbiosis at anatomically and immunologically distinct sites. For the purpose of these guidelines, mucocutaneous pyoderma is included as superficial pyoderma when lesions are similar to those found with intertrigo yet are more severe, occasionally extending to ulceration as a sign of deeper infection. Any breed can be affected, but German shepherd dogs are reported as predisposed (in contrast to intertrigo for which spaniels or any breed with prominent folds are reported as predisposed).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0019" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>19</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0083" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>83</sup>
</xref> Mucocutaneous pyoderma will also need to be differentiated from mucocutaneous lupus erythematosus (MCLE). If skin lesions remain after infection or dysbiosis have been treated successfully, histopathological evaluation from biopsy specimens will be indicated.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0084" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>84</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0038"><title>Diagnosis</title><p>Diagnosis of <bold>SBF</bold> is based on clinical and cytological (cocci and neutrophils; see Section ‘Diagnostic approach and diagnostic tests’) findings and the exclusion of other follicular diseases.</p><p>It is important to exclude demodicosis (e.g. with hair plucks or deep skin scrapings) and dermatophytosis (Wood's lamp examination; direct examination of hairs for fungal hyphae or arthrospores; fungal culture and/or dermatophyte PCR).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0044" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>44</sup>
</xref> Pemphigus foliaceus can mimic SBF, and the suspicion for this condition should be increased if lesions are seen in areas not typical for SBF (e.g. pinnal surfaces, dorsal muzzle, paw pads) or if cytology reveals numerous acantholytic keratinocytes, in addition to neutrophils and eosinophils, and few‐to‐no bacteria.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0085" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>85</sup>
</xref> Biopsies for histopathology should be considered to identify immune‐mediated, sterile pustular diseases or sometimes leishmaniosis if lesions remain after the pyoderma has been treated adequately and cytology no longer shows evidence of bacterial infection.</p><p>The large, expansile epidermal collarettes of <bold>exfoliative superficial pyoderma</bold> are visually distinctive yet should always be confirmed cytologically, following exclusion of demodicosis and dermatophytosis. Histopathology is not typically necessary for diagnosis.</p><p>
<bold>Impetigo</bold> is diagnosed based on signalment (young dog), characteristic interfollicular pustules, and a cytology that supports bacterial infection. Histopathological evaluation may be needed in rare cases to differentiate impetigo from pemphigus foliaceus. In impetigo, acantholysis is typically mild or absent, though it may be more prominent, possibly resulting from the expression of staphylococcal exotoxins capable of degrading desmosomal adhesion molecules.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0080" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>80</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0081" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>81</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0086" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>86</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0087" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>87</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>The diagnosis of <bold>mucocutaneous pyoderma</bold> is clinical and based on recognising lesions at mucocutaneous junctions combined with cytology findings supporting infection (bacteria and neutrophils). Confirmation of the diagnosis is by observation of lesion resolution on antimicrobial treatment.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0021" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>21</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0084" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>84</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0088" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>88</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0039"><title>Treatment recommendations for superficial bacterial folliculitis (<styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">SBF</styled-content>), impetigo and exfoliative superficial pyoderma</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0040"><title>Topical antimicrobial therapy</title><p>Topical antimicrobial therapy as the sole antibacterial treatment is the treatment‐of‐choice for canine superficial bacterial folliculitis (SOR A) and for other presentations of superficial pyoderma.</p><p>For <bold>SBF</bold>, topical antimicrobial therapy as the sole antibacterial treatment has been found effective in 12 of the 13 currently published clinical trials, including eight randomised controlled trials (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0008" ref-type="table">8</xref>, Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0005" ref-type="boxed-text">5</xref>). Clinical efficacy was seen for 2–4% chlorhexidine products, a 2.5% benzoyl peroxide shampoo, a sodium hypochlorite/salicylic acid shampoo, carbonated water and for two polyherbal preparations and not for stannous fluoride. Product formulations containing these active ingredients are widely available (see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’). Topical antimicrobial therapy alone also was found to be equally effective as systemic antimicrobial therapy in an RCT comparing a combination of 4% chlorhexidine digluconate shampoo and solution versus amoxicillin‐clavulanate after 4 weeks of therapy.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0089" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>89</sup>
</xref>
</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0008" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 8</label><caption><p>Published clinical trials on topical antimicrobial therapy used as the sole antimicrobial treatment for canine superficial pyoderma, identified using the search criteria of Barker et al. 2022.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0011" ref-type="bibr">

</xref>

</xref>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Chlorhexidine 2% at three different doses/area (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 9 in each group)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">RCT</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">2</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">7 days</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Majority of dogs had good response in all three groups</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Seltzer et al., 2010<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0100" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>100</sup>
</xref>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Stannous fluoride 0.2% (with glycerin &amp; zinc gluconate) (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 12) versus vehicle only (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 14), both once daily</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">RCT</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">2†</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">28 days</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">

<sup>101</sup>
</xref>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Erythritol (5%)/0.1% <sc>l</sc>‐ascorbyl‐2‐phosphate sprayed three times daily onto affected areas</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Prospective case series</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">2</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">28 days</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Clinical scores reduced in all dogs</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0005"><fn id="vde13342-note-0005"><p>Abbreviations: LoE, SORT level of evidence; RCT, randomised controlled trial.</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0006"><label>
<sup>a</sup>
</label><p>Study LoE amended for reasons given under ‘outcome’; MSSP, meticillin‐susceptible <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus pseudintermedius</italic>; MRSP, meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic>.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0005" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 5</label><caption><title>Example approach to a dog with superficial bacterial folliculitis—topical antimicrobial therapy alone (treatment‐of‐choice)</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0031"><label>1</label><p>
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0015"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0130"><p>For widespread lesions, 2%–4% chlorhexidine shampoo at least two to three times per week, 10–15 min contact time, then rinse.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0131"><p>For localised lesions, 2%–4% chlorhexidine wipes, spray or foam once daily.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0132"><p>Consider clipping affected areas in long‐coated or plush‐coated dogs.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0133"><p>Ensure adequate ectoparasite prophylaxis is in place (avoid spot‐on preparations when using frequent shampoo therapy as efficacy may be reduced)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0134"><p>Re‐assess after 2–3 weeks</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0135"><p>If progress is satisfactory, continue antiseptics until all lesions are resolved and until underlying primary causes have been identified and corrected.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0136"><p>If progress is poor, review compliance, products and primary underlying disease, then consider systemic therapy.</p></list-item></list>
</p></sec></boxed-text><p>For <bold>impetigo</bold> and <bold>exfoliative superficial pyoderma</bold>, very little information on treatment has been published. A single open noncontrolled trial showed good efficacy of 3% chlorhexidine shampoo when used twice weekly in 17 puppies with impetigo for 3 weeks.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0102" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>102</sup>
</xref> It is noted that some cases of impetigo resolve spontaneously, particularly when factors such as poor nutrition, poor sanitation and endoparasitism are corrected.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0082" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>82</sup>
</xref> Bullous impetigo in immunocompromised adult dogs is unlikely to resolve spontaneously, and it is critical that the underlying cause be found.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0002" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>2</sup>
</xref> Systemic antimicrobial therapy may need to be considered if lesions do not respond to topical therapy alone.</p><p>Response to topical therapy should be re‐assessed by a veterinarian after 2–3 weeks.</p><p>Clinical trials have demonstrated lesion resolution in most dogs after 3–4 weeks of therapy, with lesion improvement occurring within 1–2 weeks of starting therapy.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0089" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>89</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0093" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>93</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0094" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>94</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0096" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>96</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0097" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>97</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0098" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>98</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0099" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>99</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0103" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>103</sup>
</xref> Topical antiseptics are then maintained until all lesions are resolved <italic toggle="yes">and</italic> underlying primary causes are identified and addressed.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0041"><title>When is systemic antimicrobial therapy indicated for <styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">SBF</styled-content>?</title><p>We found no clinical reasons or indications in published studies that would justify the selection of systemic over topical antimicrobial therapy alone for superficial pyoderma. However, if the dog's temperament, owner's ability or compliance or available facilities make topical treatment impossible, or if lesions have not improved sufficiently after 2 weeks of topical therapy alone, systemic antimicrobials will need to be considered. Nevertheless, before starting systemic antimicrobials, a re‐evaluation for persistent concurrent disease(s) that may impair resolution should be made.</p><p>Systemic antimicrobial therapy should be reserved for cases that have failed to respond to topical antimicrobial therapy alone or if topical therapy is not feasible due to client or patient limitations.</p><p>BC/AST and cytology should be used whenever possible to guide systemic drug choices.</p><p>Drugs should only be chosen empirically when the risk for meticillin resistance is deemed low, and only first‐choice agents (clindamycin, cefalexin, cefadroxil, amoxicillin‐clavulanate) should be considered for empirical selection.</p><p>Based on in vitro and in vivo studies, first‐choice agents should be effective against the majority of meticillin‐susceptible <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> (MSSP). However, minor variations in susceptibilities occur even in MSSP so that BC/AST is always justified to guide selection of the most appropriate drug for a particular dog.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0008" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>8</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0104" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>104</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0105" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>105</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0106" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>106</sup>
</xref> BC/AST will be particularly helpful and is likely to be cost‐effective in areas with a high prevalence of MRS, as expenses resulting from ineffective treatment can be avoided. For recommendations on dosages see Section ‘Systemic antimicrobial therapy’ and Tables <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0014" ref-type="table">14</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0015" ref-type="table">15</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0016" ref-type="table">16</xref>.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0042"><title>How long is it to treat superficial pyoderma when using systemic antimicrobial therapy?</title><p>An initial 2‐week course may be dispensed, and an appointment for re‐examination by a veterinarian should be scheduled before the end of the course to determine whether systemic treatment can be stopped or whether longer treatment is required (SOR C).</p><p>Published clinical trials available for assessing minimum treatment duration for SBF utilised varying outcome measures, methods of lesion assessment, re‐evaluation times and definitions of clinical resolution.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0008" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>8</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0107" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>107</sup>
</xref> While some studies included dogs with SBF as well as dogs with deep pyoderma, others assessed treatment over a 2‐ to 4‐week period yet did not always include a 2‐week assessment (Tables <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0002" ref-type="supplementary-material">S2</xref> and <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0004" ref-type="supplementary-material">S4</xref>).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0108" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>108</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0109" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>109</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0110" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>110</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0111" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>111</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0112" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>112</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0113" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>113</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0114" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>114</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0115" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>115</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0116" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>116</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0117" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>117</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0118" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>118</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0119" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>119</sup>
</xref> Available data indicate that while not all cases of superficial pyoderma resolve within 2 weeks of systemic antimicrobials, a substantial proportion of dogs will show full clinical response at 2 weeks. Also in support of shorter treatment durations is the result from a field trial used for drug approval and now available through the Freedom of Information Summaries (FOI) from the US Food &amp; Drug Administration (FDA) (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="http://www.fda.gov" ext-link-type="uri">www.FDA.gov</ext-link>). In an FDA‐approved trial, 94.8% (118 cases) and 91.2% (117 cases) of dogs receiving cefovecin and cefadroxil, respectively, for the treatment of SBF were considered cured, based on examination for clinical signs of infection, 14 days after beginning treatment.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0120" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>120</sup>
</xref> Our recommendation of shorter antimicrobial courses is further supported by a similar trend in human medicine where there is already good evidence for successful treatment outcomes with shorter treatment durations in the management of skin and soft tissue infections; similar veterinary studies are needed.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0121" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>121</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0122" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>122</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Where a re‐examination before the end of the initial 2‐week course is not feasible, treatment can be extended to avoid stopping treatment before veterinary assessment. The required treatment duration for systemic therapy in SBF will vary depending on contributing clinical factors and primary underlying disease. However, in view of the urgent need to reduce unnecessary antimicrobial use and following a review of the published literature specifically on treatment duration for SBF, the authors consider that the historically advocated 3–4 weeks duration can no longer be justified and should be replaced by an initial 2‐week course combined with re‐examination while still receiving systemic therapy to determine whether treatment extensions are indicated.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0108" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>108</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0109" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>109</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0110" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>110</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0111" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>111</sup>
</xref> Clients should be carefully instructed to monitor for lesion improvement at home during the treatment period. Improvement of SBF lesions with systemic therapy should start within 5–7 days of instituting appropriate antimicrobial therapy; if lesions are not improving within this time frame, the dog should be reassessed earlier than planned by a veterinarian so that BC/AST and cytology can be implemented to re‐assess therapy. More complicated or chronic cases may require longer treatments, as, reportedly, might cases involving MRS.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0091" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>91</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>A desire for ‘just in case’ longer prescribing to maximise good outcomes may be understandable but can be counterbalanced and replaced by more frequent monitoring of progress in order to minimise unnecessary antimicrobial use. Scheduled and more frequent re‐examinations will provide opportunities for further diagnostics of underlying primary conditions, and for early detection of drug resistance if progress is poor. It is important to recognise that inflammation can persist beyond resolution of infection; if erythema remains, overlong antimicrobial therapy can be avoided if cytological findings no longer show evidence of bacterial infection.</p><p>Adjunctive topical antimicrobial therapy is recommended whenever possible (SOR C).</p><p>Adjunctive topical antimicrobial therapy may shorten the duration of systemic treatment required. In one open controlled trial with 20 dogs receiving cefalexin for 14 days, severity indices were lower for the 10 dogs that received adjunctive ethyl lactate bathing, as compared to those treated only systemically.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0123" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>123</sup>
</xref> In another study using cefalexin for superficial pyoderma, a notable reduction of clinical scores was seen after 21 days in dogs that received a polyherbal topical preparation concurrently compared to after 28 days in the control dogs.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0103" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>103</sup>
</xref> Fluorescent light therapy used alone has been shown to achieve shorter durations to resolution in a group of 12 dogs compared to eight receiving systemic cefadroxil for their superficial bacterial folliculitis.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0124" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>124</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Clinical resolution of superficial pyoderma can be assumed, and systemic antimicrobials stopped when primary lesions of pyoderma (papules, pustules and erythematous epidermal collarettes) are no longer found.</p><p>The best way to determine the cure of superficial pyoderma (and to decide when to stop systemic antimicrobial therapy) is currently unknown, partly because ‘sterility’ of the skin cannot be used as a measure for treatment success. Following successful treatment, cytology from healed skin will show mainly squames (keratinocytes) while culture can still yield normal bacterial flora, including staphylococci. In cases without primary lesions, where, for example easily epilating hairs may be the only visible clinical signs, the evaluation of clinical resolution may be more difficult but can be supported by cytology. Furthermore, remaining clinical signs may be caused by underlying primary causes (e.g. erythema owing to allergic disease) or they may be secondary lesions related to pyoderma and slow to resolve (e.g. patchy alopecia remaining for ≤6 weeks after papules, pustules and epidermal collarettes have healed).</p><p>Topical antiseptic treatment can be continued longer than systemic therapy, and is potentially life‐long, where the primary causes cannot be resolved and the risk of recurrence remains.</p><p>There is no evidence to support extending systemic antimicrobial therapy beyond the resolution of clinical signs associated with infection; instead, underlying primary causes must be identified and addressed.</p><p>The frustrating tendency of canine SBF to frequently recur has previously led to the recommendation of extending treatment for 1 week beyond clinical cure with the intention to reduce the risk of recurrence.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0107" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>107</sup>
</xref> In the absence of supportive data, such prophylactic systemic therapy cannot be recommended and should be replaced with proactive topical antimicrobial therapy and intensified diagnostic searches for underlying causes (Section ‘Preventing recurrences of pyoderma’).</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0043"><title>Treatment recommendations for mucocutaneous pyoderma</title><p>Topical antimicrobial therapy alone is the treatment‐of‐choice for most cases of canine mucocutaneous pyoderma. Topical therapy in the form of gel, ointment or cream preparations is recommended (see Section ‘Product formulations’). For cases that present with extensive ulceration and pain, and for those that have failed to respond to topical antimicrobial therapy alone, systemic antimicrobials may be considered.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0083" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>83</sup>
</xref> Where systemic antimicrobial therapy is considered, drug selection should always be based on BC/AST results as the involved pathogens may be diverse and show variable susceptibilities (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0006" ref-type="boxed-text">6</xref>).</p><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0006" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 6</label><caption><title>Example approach to a dog with mucocutaneous pyoderma</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0035"><label>1</label><p>
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0016"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0137"><p>Treat the bacterial infection topically, guided by cytological findings.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0138"><p>Prioritise topical antimicrobial therapy with gel, ointment or cream preparations over more liquid solutions to avoid mechanical removal.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0139"><p>If erosions or ulcers are noticed, the use of hypochlorous acid products should be considered owing to their safety on open wounds.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0140"><p>Re‐assess after 2–3 weeks</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0141"><p>If lesions (e.g. erosions, ulcers, crusts) remain
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0017"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0142"><p>and cytological evidence of infection persists, consider adding systemic antimicrobial therapy</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0143"><p>following cytological clearance of infection, consider taking biopsies for histopathological evaluation to investigate other differentials such as leishmaniosis or discoid or mucocutaneous lupus erythematosus.</p></list-item></list>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0144"><p>Investigate, correct or manage underlying primary causes</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0145"><p>If lesions present as chronically relapsing, consider proactive therapy, for example daily 2–4% chlorhexidine wipes.</p></list-item></list>
</p></sec></boxed-text></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0044"><label>7</label><title>DEEP PYODERMA</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0045"><title>Clinical presentations</title><p>Deep pyoderma is a bacterial infection occurring in the dermis and sometimes extending into the subcutaneous tissue (panniculus). It includes a heterogeneous group of different clinical presentations which can be widespread or localised (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0009" ref-type="table">9</xref>, Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0008" ref-type="fig">8</xref>). In localised presentations, the terminology for the disease typically relates to the site of the primary clinical entity.</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0009" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 9</label><caption><p>Clinical presentations of deep pyoderma and typical associated clinical findings.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" colspan="2" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Presentation</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Typical skin lesions</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Distribution</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Differential diagnoses</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comments</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">Widespread/generalised</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Deep folliculitis and furunculosis (cellulitis)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">

sternum of short‐coated, deep‐chested dogs</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Chin pyoderma (‘chin acne’)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Papules, ± ulceration, erythema, haemopurulent exudate (bleeding if vibrissae are affected)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Chin, muzzle, lips, ventral neck</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Demodicosis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Likely primary causes aggravated by, for example trauma (rubbing). Mostly young dogs and short‐haired breeds</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0008" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 8</label><caption><p>Examples of deep pyoderma in dogs. (a) Chin pyoderma (‘chin acne’). (b) Interdigital infected nodule. (c) Acral lick dermatitis. (d) Widespread deep folliculitis and furunculosis.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-17" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g007.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g007.jpg?><?image-size 194010?><?image-md5 aaf43c5a147cad3061fa1a93bac00a42?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 939?><?image-original-width 1064?><?image-scaled-height 626?><?image-scaled-width 709?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/aaf43c5a147c/VDE-36-234-g007.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g007.gif?><?thumb-size 8962?><?thumb-md5 d1da70258b423dd4f9fbc12019082130?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 88?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/d1da70258b42/VDE-36-234-g007.gif?></graphic></fig><p>The proximity of bacterial infection to dermal blood vessels makes deep pyoderma a potentially serious and debilitating disease with a risk of haematogenous spread and progression to septicaemia.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0125" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>125</sup>
</xref> Infected areas that involve marked, diffuse inflammation (cellulitis) are frequently painful so that signs of self‐trauma may be absent. In presentations where irritation or discomfort is part of the underlying primary problem, self‐trauma can be intense, for example in acral lick dermatitis (ALD). If deep infection is severe, lesions may heal with scarring.</p><p>Skin lesions indicative of deep pyoderma include haemorrhagic crusts and haemopurulent discharge, denoting an extension of infection into the dermal skin layer where blood vessels reside. Also suggestive of deep pyoderma are furuncles, which are defined as infected, ruptured hair follicles that appear as large (&gt;3 mm diameter) red‐to‐bluish papules from which pus can be expressed. Although the term ‘furuncle’ was formative in the naming of important diseases, its use (similar to that of ‘carbuncle’ for infection that spans several adjacent hair follicles) has become blurred in the recent veterinary literature. Nodules, ill‐defined swellings, fistulae and sinuses also can be associated with deep pyoderma. Ulcers may occur with infectious and noninfectious processes, yet their presence in a dog with bacterial infection indicates involvement of deeper skin layers and thus deep pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0009" ref-type="table">9</xref>). Necrotic changes may follow in severe advanced cases and should prompt urgency. The umbrella terms ‘skin and soft tissue infections’ or ‘cellulitis’ are commonly used in human medicine when referring to deep bacterial skin infections, but differentiation of the specific presentation in dogs has merit as management varies, particularly that of underlying primary causes.</p><p>The pathogenesis of deep infections is largely presumed. An extension of bacterial folliculitis into furunculosis via rupture of infected hair follicles has been proposed as a likely pathogenesis for widespread deep pyoderma, while some localised lesions may occur following traumatic processes (e.g. cuts, bites and penetrating foreign bodies, including hair shaft fragments) that introduce bacteria into deeper layers. The course of the disease then largely depends on the host's ability to mount an appropriate immune response, and contain and resolve infection. Infection can spread along tissue planes and progress gradually or explosively. The latter is more typical for infections involving pathogens such as β‐haemolytic <italic toggle="yes">Streptococcus canis</italic>, for example in necrotising fasciitis.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0126" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>126</sup>
</xref> Histopathologically, the inflammatory infiltrate in deep pyoderma is predominantly neutrophilic or pyogranulomatous, and focussed around hair follicles and adnexa.</p><p>Deep bacterial skin infections that are not included in these guidelines are anaerobic cellulitis, infections caused by specific agents causing a nodular or diffuse (pyo)granulomatous dermatitis and panniculitis (e.g. botryomycosis, mycobacterial granulomas) and subcutaneous abscesses.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0127" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>127</sup>
</xref> Information on approaches to these rarer diseases can be found elsewhere.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0002" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>2</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0003" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>3</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>
<bold>German shepherd dog deep pyoderma (GSP)</bold> as a distinct entity was purposefully omitted from the list of deep pyoderma presentations in these guidelines. GSP was previously recognised as a very severe and widespread deep pyoderma almost exclusively affecting some middle‐aged German shepherd dogs (GSDs) and their crosses.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0128" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>128</sup>
</xref> The condition was slow to respond to treatment and prone to recurrence. A familial background could be shown<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0129" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>129</sup>
</xref> and careful breeding may have led to the perceived reduced frequency of this syndrome over the last 10 years. Until specific characteristics that are helpful for the diagnosis and management are identified, deep pyoderma in a GSD should be treated in the same way as deep pyoderma in any other breed.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0046"><title>Diagnosis</title><p>Deep pyoderma is diagnosed based on the identification of compatible skin lesions such as draining tracts, haemorrhagic crusts, purple papules, ulceration or swelling and confirmation of a bacterial cause through cytological evaluation of representative areas. Clipping of long or matted hair may be needed to appreciate lesions. In some cases (e.g. ALD), signs typical of bacterial infection (e.g. pus, pustules) may be less obvious and deep pyoderma needs to be investigated based on clinical suspicion.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0056" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>56</sup>
</xref> In some cases, invasive sampling from below an intact surface is needed, either by biopsy or by needle aspiration (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0001" ref-type="boxed-text">1</xref>).</p><p>Underlying primary causes include all those described for other pyodermas (see Section ‘Investigations into underlying primary causes’). Atopic disease is likely to be the most common cause, yet demodicosis, hypothyroidism, hyperadrenocorticism and neoplastic processes also should be considered, depending on the case presentation. Inquiry into the dog's environment is an integral part of the diagnostic process in cases of ALD, callus pyoderma and interdigital infected nodules.</p><p>With paw lesions, examination (including palpation) of the palmar/plantar aspects of the affected paw(s) is critical to identify fused pads or comedones on weight‐bearing, haired surfaces. Identification of excess body weight or obesity and of conformational or gait abnormalities or joint disease (via orthopaedic examination and potentially including imaging if infectious osteomyelitis is suspected) is recommended.</p><sec id="vde13342-sec-0047"><title>
<styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">BC</styled-content>/<styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">AST</styled-content> for deep pyoderma</title><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus pseudintermedius</italic> remains the major pathogen and accounts for approximately 60% of pathogens isolated from deep pyoderma, which is in contrast to the &gt;90% isolated from superficial pyoderma. Nevertheless, infections involving Gram‐negative organisms (e.g. <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">Pseudomonas</italic> spp.) or mixed bacterial populations are not uncommon. Although anaerobic pathogens are rarely reported, an anaerobic culture should always be included when processing samples from cases of deep pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0010" ref-type="table">10</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0010" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 10</label><caption><p>Bacterial pathogens most commonly reported from dogs with deep pyoderma (Web of Science, search string on 19 April 2022 using the string: Dog AND (furunculosis OR deep pyoderma OR cellulitis OR interdigital pyoderma OR acral lick) AND (culture OR microbiology OR aetiology OR bacteria). The last three papers in the table were retrieved from other sources.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" rowspan="2" valign="bottom" colspan="1">Reference</th><th colspan="2" align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Number of</th><th align="left" rowspan="2" valign="bottom" colspan="1">Deep pyoderma type</th><th align="left" colspan="2" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Staphylococci</th><th align="left" rowspan="2" valign="bottom" colspan="1">

</xref>
</td><td align="char" char="." valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">61</td><td align="char" char="." valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">36</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Interdigital nodules</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">26</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">6</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">18</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">8</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">3,<xref rid="vde13342-note-0014" ref-type="table-fn">
