</xref>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Špruçek et al. 2007<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0135" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>135</sup>
</xref>
</td><td align="char" char="." valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">10</td><td align="char" char="." valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">10</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Unspecified deep pyoderma</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">7</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1,<xref rid="vde13342-note-0016" ref-type="table-fn">
<sup>i</sup>
</xref>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Total</td><td align="char" char="." valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">428</td><td align="char" char="." valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">285</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1"/><td align="left" colspan="2" valign="top" rowspan="1">270 (63%)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">47 (11%)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">18 (4%)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">39 (9%)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">25 (6%)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">39 (9%)</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0006"><fn id="vde13342-note-0007"><p>Abbreviation: SP, <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus pseudintermedius</italic>.</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0008"><label>
<sup>a</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Enterobacter</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 6).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0009"><label>
<sup>b</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Pantoea</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 9), <italic toggle="yes">Acinetobacter</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 5) and <italic toggle="yes">Pasteurella</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 4).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0010"><label>
<sup>c</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Enterobacter</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 3).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0011"><label>
<sup>d</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Clostridium perfringens</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0012"><label>
<sup>e</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Enterobacter cloacae</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1), <italic toggle="yes">Serratia marcescens</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1), <italic toggle="yes">Klebsiella oxytoca</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0013"><label>
<sup>f</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Burkholderia cepacia</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0014"><label>
<sup>g</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Bacillus</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 3).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0015"><label>
<sup>h</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Klebsiella</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 4).</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0016"><label>
<sup>i</sup>
</label><p>
<italic toggle="yes">Actinomyces</italic> (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1).</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>This higher pathogen diversity in deep pyoderma has important implications for antimicrobial therapy because some antimicrobials recommended for treatment of staphylococci‐associated pyoderma are inactive (e.g. owing to inherent resistance to lincosamides) or have limited efficacy against certain Gram‐negative bacteria such as <italic toggle="yes">Pseudomonas</italic> or AmpC‐producers (e.g. amoxicillin‐clavulanate). For other drugs, including cefalexin and other first‐generation cephalosporins, tetracyclines (doxycycline) and cefovecin, clinical breakpoints are approved for testing Gram‐negative bacteria from dogs, yet most isolates will be reported as resistant because concentrations achievable with standard doses are insufficiently high. Therefore, some laboratories may not include these agents in a culture report (each laboratory has the discretion to decide what agents to include in a report). This highlights the importance of submitting samples for BC/AST to laboratories that follow recognised standards (see Section ‘How to interpret BC/AST reports from dogs with pyoderma’).</p><p>Samples for BC/AST must be representative of the deep infection as surface swabs will often miss the relevant pathogens (see Section ‘Bacterial culture and antimicrobial susceptibility testing (BC/AST)’ and Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0004" ref-type="table">4</xref>). In order to maximise the benefit of BC/AST results, cytology should always be performed from the same lesion so that culture results can be matched to the predominant pathogen morphology (e.g. cocci, rods or mixed) as seen cytologically, and thus inform the best drug choice (see Section ‘Cytological examination’).</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0048"><title>Treatment recommendations for deep pyoderma</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0049"><title>Antimicrobial therapy</title><p>Systemic antibacterial therapy is always indicated in deep pyoderma, and the choice of drug should always be based on BC/AST results.</p><p>Culture‐based prescribing is particularly important in deep pyoderma to reduce the risk of treatment failure of <bold>systemic antimicrobials</bold> and of severe clinical consequences. Furthermore, owing to the long treatment periods typically required for deep pyoderma, the cost of laboratory testing will most often be counterbalanced by the cost of potentially ineffective empirically chosen medication.</p><p>Our literature search found 33 published clinical trials involving dogs with deep pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0003" ref-type="supplementary-material">S3</xref>) and another 16 investigating dogs with deep and superficial pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0004" ref-type="supplementary-material">S4</xref>). Systemic antimicrobial therapy had been evaluated in all, with adjunctive topical therapy in 16 (either as part of the study design or allowed to be continued from before).</p><p>Provided that BC/AST has indicated susceptibility, first‐choice drugs (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0014" ref-type="table">14</xref>) should be considered before second‐choice agents (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0015" ref-type="table">15</xref>). Choices will also be guided by clinical characteristics (accumulation in skin, penetration into inflamed tissue, activity in pus, safety, previous adverse drug reactions in the individual dog), and general considerations such as formulation availability, dosing frequency and cost.</p><p>Systemic antimicrobials are best started when BC/AST results are available. However, if there is a high risk of deterioration or septicaemia, empirical treatment guided by cytological findings can be started immediately, with re‐evaluation of drug selection when laboratory results are received.</p><p>Adjunctive topical antimicrobial therapy is recommended in every case as soon as the dog is considered pain free (SOR C).</p><p>Although no studies specifically investigated the effect of <bold>adjunctive topical antimicrobial therapy</bold> in deep pyoderma, adjunctive topical antimicrobials were used in 10 of the 30 published deep pyoderma studies, at least for some of the dogs (Table <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0003" ref-type="supplementary-material">S3</xref>). Topical therapy is expected to facilitate healing by removing crust, reducing the microbial load on the surface, reducing pathogens and possibly shortening the duration of systemic therapy required. For the selection of active ingredients, product formulations and tips on maximising efficacy, see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0050"><title>How long to treat deep pyoderma and when to stop systemic antimicrobials</title><p>An initial 3‐week course may be dispensed, and an appointment for re‐examination by a veterinarian should be scheduled before the end of the course to determine whether systemic treatment can be stopped or whether longer treatment is required.</p><p>If clinical signs are improving but have not resolved, and if cytological evidence of infection is still present, treatment should be continued with re‐evaluation every 2 weeks.</p><p>Systemic antimicrobial therapy can be stopped when skin lesions associated with deep infection (draining/fistulous tracts, pus, pustules, crusts) have resolved and there is no cytological evidence of infection.</p><p>At re‐examination, if draining/fistulous tracts, pus, pustules, ulcers or crusts remain or if lesions are no longer improving (have plateaued), their bacterial nature should be confirmed again by cytology to differentiate ongoing infection from sterile disease processes; if bacteria are seen on cytology, BC/AST need to be repeated to investigate drug resistance.</p><p>Topical antiseptic treatment can be continued beyond stopping systemic therapy where the primary underlying cause(s) cannot be resolved and the risk of recurrence remains.</p><p>There is no evidence to support extending systemic antimicrobial therapy beyond the resolution of clinical signs; instead, underlying primary causes must be identified and addressed.</p><p>High‐quality data from clinical treatment trials in canine deep pyoderma are sparse and conclusions on best treatment duration are limited by substantial variation in study design (Tables <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0003" ref-type="supplementary-material">S3</xref> and <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0004" ref-type="supplementary-material">S4</xref>). Traditionally, a minimum of 4 weeks, extending to 8–12 weeks and 2 weeks beyond clinical cure were recommended.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0002" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>2</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0016" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>16</sup>
</xref> Several months of antimicrobial treatment have been mentioned for the management of ALD.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0136" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>136</sup>
</xref> The rationale behind longer courses for deep pyoderma was to avoid sequestered, subclinical foci of infection causing relapse, yet evidence to support this practice is lacking. With these long treatment recommendations under scrutiny and following our literature review, our group's consensus was to shorten the recommendations by advocating 3 weeks as the initial duration, supported by adjunctive topical antimicrobials and scheduled re‐examinations every 2 weeks to monitor progress. Re‐examinations provide an opportunity to stop, extend or adjust treatment depending on progress, to review compliance and safety of treatment, and to investigate and manage the underlying primary causes. Where a re‐examination before the initial 3 week course is not feasible, treatment can be extended to avoid stopping treatment before veterinary assessment.</p><p>At re‐examination, when lesions no longer improve, previously affected areas should be sampled for cytology again (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0004" ref-type="table">4</xref>) to establish whether they are still caused by bacterial infection. If intracellular bacteria are still seen in cytology findings and clinical progress has stopped, another sample should be taken for BC/AST to investigate drug resistance. If appropriately taken samples no longer show cytological evidence of bacterial infection, persistent lesions may be attributed to underlying primary, potentially chronic disease and sterile diseases (Clinical case scenario in Section ‘Clinical case scenario’).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0137" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>137</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0138" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>138</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>The consensus is that it is very rare that &gt;6 weeks of appropriate antimicrobial therapy will be required to eliminate the secondary pyoderma component of skin lesions. Although it will remain difficult to show that bacteria are no longer contributing to remaining lesions (e.g. where remnants of dead <italic toggle="yes">Demodex</italic> mites or hair‐shaft‐induced cysts arising from the palmar/plantar paw surfaces continue to drive inflammation), anti‐inflammatory medications such as prednisolone or ciclosporin, as clinically appropriate for the case, should be considered to replace antimicrobials (Clinical case scenario in Section ‘Clinical case scenario’). Healing of chronic lesions with scarring (fibrosis) may leave affected skin permanently alopecic and more fragile, yet further improvement and prevention of recurrent infection will not require systemic antimicrobials.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0051"><title>Supportive management</title><p>Medication to <bold>relieve pain</bold> should be considered if the dog is likely to be in pain.</p><p>In order to support the resolution of infection during antimicrobial treatment and to prevent recurrence after infection has resolved, <bold>underlying primary causes</bold> must be found and controlled. In many cases, this will be a bigger challenge than resolving the pyoderma component. However, the importance of resolving the underlying primary cause should not be underestimated as individual cases of deep pyoderma have even been shown to improve without systemic antimicrobial therapy. In one study, 16 dogs with generalised demodicosis and secondary bacterial furunculosis achieved complete resolution with acaricidal treatment and benzoyl peroxide washes alone<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0139" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>139</sup>
</xref>; guidelines for the treatment of demodicosis have been published.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0140" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>140</sup>
</xref> Likewise, a single case of severe postgrooming furunculosis, confirmed histologically, is described to have fully recovered with once daily anti‐inflammatory doses of prednisolone alone.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0133" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>133</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Adjunctive <bold>fluorescence biomodulation</bold> has been shown to shorten the time to lesion resolution in dogs with deep pyoderma and interdigital furunculosis<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0134" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>134</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0141" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>141</sup>
</xref> and it has also been reported to have led to resolution of MDR pyoderma in 16 dogs.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0142" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>142</sup>
</xref> This effect may not be the result of a direct antimicrobial action of the light therapy and, rather, of other mechanisms that support healing and restoration of skin barrier function.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0143" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>143</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Whirlpool therapy was historically and anecdotally recommended as adjunctive therapy to promote healing, yet following the emergence of multidrug‐resistant pathogens, infection control measures would need to be rigorous.</p><p>
<bold>Management changes</bold> should be tailored to the disease presentation, underlying primary cause(s), body site and practicality for owners. For example, providing softer surfaces for play and rest and facilitating weight loss in overweight dogs may reduce friction and chronic trauma to vulnerable and affected areas (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0007" ref-type="boxed-text">7</xref>).</p><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0007" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 7</label><caption><title>Example treatment approach to a dog with widespread deep bacterial folliculitis‐furunculosis</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0036"><label>1</label><p>
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0018"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0146"><p>Dispense 3 weeks of systemic antimicrobial therapy, selected based on bacterial culture and antimicrobial susceptibility test results (prioritising first‐choice drugs over second‐choice drugs if susceptibility is indicated)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0147"><p>Adjunctive topical antimicrobial therapy (e.g. 2%–4% chlorhexidine shampoo, solution (spray) or mousse) to be applied every 24–48 h</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0148"><p>If the dog is in pain, consider adequate pain‐relief medication</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0149"><p>Identify, investigate and address underlying primary causes</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0150"><p>Re‐assess progress before the end of the 3‐week course:
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0019"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0151"><p>If all skin lesions have resolved: stop systemic antimicrobials</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0152"><p>If lesions are improving but lesions related to pyoderma remain (e.g. diffuse swelling, papules, pustules, furuncles or pus can still be expressed) <bold>and cytology still reveals intracellular bacteria</bold>: continue systemic antimicrobials for another 2 weeks and schedule re‐examination in 2 weeks (before the end of the course). This step can be repeated every 2 weeks until resolution of infection.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0153"><p>Consider whether anti‐inflammatory drugs (e.g. glucocorticoids or ciclosporin) are indicated and clinically appropriate to replace antimicrobials and support healing (see Clinical case scenario 6.4)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0154"><p>If the lesions have not improved or worsened, (1) reassess compliance, (2) take skin biopsies for histopathological evaluation and BC/AST and (3) review investigations into underlying causes</p></list-item></list>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0155"><p>Ensure that a long‐term plan for correcting or managing underlying primary causes is in place before stopping topical antiseptic therapy.</p></list-item></list>
</p></sec></boxed-text></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0052"><title>Clinical case scenario</title><p>A 4‐year‐old, spayed female Mastiff cross presents with a 2.5‐year history of generalised pruritic dermatitis. Improvement had always been seen with intermittent treatment using oral prednisone, oclacitinib and systemic antimicrobials (cefalexin, amoxicillin‐clavulanate), yet skin lesions always recurred when treatment was stopped. At presentation, she showed signs of pruritus, symmetrical areas of alopecia and hyperpigmentation on the face, neck and flanks (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0009" ref-type="fig">9a</xref>) and alopecia, erythema, papules, pustules and suppurative fistulous tracts on all four paws (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0009" ref-type="fig">9b</xref>) with signs of pain when walking.</p><fig position="float" fig-type="FIGURE" id="vde13342-fig-0009" orientation="portrait"><label>FIGURE 9</label><caption><p>Mastiff cross with deep pyoderma secondary to allergic skin disease. (a) Widespread alopecia and hyperpigmentation affecting the body. (b) Severe suppurative pododermatitis on all four paws.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="jats-graphic-19" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="VDE-36-234-g008.jpg"><?image-name VDE-36-234-g008.jpg?><?image-size 180878?><?image-md5 5dd0e49e958a0290dfe3b34fde4b0f2d?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1689?><?image-original-width 932?><?image-scaled-height 1125?><?image-scaled-width 621?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/5dd0e49e958a/VDE-36-234-g008.jpg?><?thumb-name VDE-36-234-g008.gif?><?thumb-size 15704?><?thumb-md5 0074fe46e50335186da97ea1b2744540?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 181?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/dc3f/12058580/0074fe46e503/VDE-36-234-g008.gif?></graphic></fig><p>Microscopic examination of plucked hairs (trichogram) showed no evidence of <italic toggle="yes">Demodex</italic> mites or fungal elements. Wood's lamp examination also was negative. Cytology from impression smears revealed pyogranulomatous inflammation with the presence of intra‐ and extracellular cocci. A diagnosis of deep pyoderma (folliculitis‐furunculosis) secondary to allergic dermatitis (atopic dermatitis or adverse reaction to food or both) was made.</p><p>After careful cleaning of the surface of a paw lesion, first with water and then with 70% alcohol, a swab for BC/AST was obtained from the exudate discharging from the interdigital fistulous tracts. This yielded an abundant, pure growth of a meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> (MRSC), resistant to all antimicrobials in the first‐choice group. Susceptibility was reported to all fluoroquinolones tested, and enrofloxacin (10 mg/kg per os once daily) was prescribed. Additionally, an oral isoxazoline, pain relief medication (7 days), and 3% chlorhexidine whole‐body washes every 48 h were prescribed, more frequently for the paws.</p><p>Three weeks later, all suppurative interdigital lesions had healed, although areas of alopecia and scarring remained. Enrofloxacin treatment was discontinued, yet it was recommended to continue with two chlorhexidine baths per week. A food trial was declined, so it was recommended to start treatment with ciclosporin 5 mg/kg p.o. once daily for the long‐term control of the allergic dermatitis. At re‐examination 2 months later, all skin lesions had resolved and no signs of recurrence had been noted. Ciclosporin therapy at the lowest necessary frequency combined with once or twice weekly chlorhexidine washes was recommended to be continued long‐term.</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0053"><label>8</label><title>TOPICAL ANTIMICROBIAL THERAPY</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0054"><title>General comments</title><p>Topical therapy used as sole antimicrobial treatment is the treatment‐of‐choice for all cases of surface and superficial pyoderma (SOR A) and also should be considered as adjunct therapy in all cases of pyoderma that require systemic treatment.</p><p>Topical antimicrobial therapy provides a great opportunity for responsible antimicrobial stewardship when managing patients with pyoderma (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0008" ref-type="boxed-text">8</xref>).</p><boxed-text position="float" content-type="BOX" id="vde13342-fea-0008" orientation="portrait"><label>BOX 8</label><caption><title>Advantages of topical antibacterial treatment</title></caption><sec sec-type="opening-section" id="vde13342-sec-0037"><label>1</label><p>Reduces need for/use of systemic antimicrobials and reduces impact of systemic antimicrobial therapy on gut microbiome, as well as other adverse events.</p><p>Effective in the treatment of surface and superficial pyoderma; can replace systemic antimicrobials.</p><p>When used as adjunctive therapy, it may reduce the duration of systemic therapy needed.</p><p>Effective against both meticillin‐susceptible and meticillin‐resistant staphylococci.</p><p>Can rapidly reduce environmental contamination by MRSP, MRSA, MRSC by reducing carriage on squames and hair.</p><p>Low risk of driving antimicrobial resistance when used appropriately owing to high local concentration of the active ingredient at the site of infection.</p><p>Safe, usually well‐tolerated and suitable for long‐term use.</p><p>Often cost‐effective.</p><p>Shampoo or wipe formulations: reduce malodour, scale and crust, number of bacteria and biofilm and increase cleanliness.</p></sec></boxed-text><p>For best outcomes, clinicians need to choose an active ingredient(s) effective against the causative microbes and a product formulation suitable for the clinical presentation and for the dog–owner combination.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0055"><title>Which active ingredient?</title><p>Topical antimicrobials are from diverse groups of agents with sometimes overlapping definitions (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0011" ref-type="table">11</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0011" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 11</label><caption><p>Selected terms and definitions used in this document in the context of topical agents that can inhibit or kill bacteria.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Group</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Definition</th><th align="left" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Example</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Antibiotic</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Derived from a micro‐organism. Some antibiotics used topically also may be used systemically, yet possibly not in all species.</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Fusidic acid, mupirocin, polymyxin B</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Antimicrobial</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Produced chemically or derived from a micro‐organism.</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Silver sulfadiazine</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Antiseptic</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Applied to living tissue, for example to skin.</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Chlorhexidine</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Biocide</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Diverse group, sometimes including disinfectants, but also pest control products or preservatives.</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Various</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Disinfectant</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Used on inanimate objects. Some are used in much lower concentrations as antiseptics.</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Sodium hypochlorite (bleach)</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap><p>Antiseptics should be prioritised over topical antibiotics. Topical antibiotics should be reserved for cases that have not responded to antiseptics or if antiseptics are deemed inappropriate (e.g. known hypersensitivities). Although evidence for this concept is lacking at present, using topical antibiotics prudently may preserve their efficacy for systemic treatment in animals or humans.</p><p>A large body of data has been published showing good in vitro anti‐staphylococcal activity for many topically used agents, while comparatively fewer clinical treatment studies are available on efficacy in vivo, such as in the presence of hair, organic material, and biofilm. Active ingredients for which clinical studies were found during our systematic literature review are listed in Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0012" ref-type="table">12</xref> (and Table <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0001" ref-type="supplementary-material">S1</xref>). Additionally, some agents not listed in the tables may be of use for the management of bacterial skin infections based on in vitro support, non‐English language studies, surgical scrub models or anecdote. Alternative agents with potential for topical antimicrobial therapy in canine pyoderma are undergoing testing and may become available for clinical use in the future.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0144" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>144</sup>
</xref>
</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0012" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 12</label><caption><p>Recommendations for use of selected active ingredients as topical antibacterial therapy in canine surface and superficial pyoderma based on evidence for efficacy from in vivo studies in dogs with spontaneous surface or superficial pyoderma identified during a systemic literature review.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0011" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>11</sup>

<sup>75</sup>
</xref>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">–</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Summers et al.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0008" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>8</sup>
</xref> reported limited evidence for efficacy in intertrigo based on a report published in Swedish.</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap><p>While the clinical role of biofilm produced by <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic>
<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0145" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>145</sup>
</xref> in pyoderma remains unknown, physical disruption of potential biofilms and removal of surface debris using water and wipes or shampoos is likely to improve the efficacy of topical antimicrobials.</p><sec id="vde13342-sec-0056"><title>Antiseptics</title><p>Currently, the agent with the greatest support for efficacy in canine pyoderma is chlorhexidine at 2–4%, available and found effective in several compositions and formulations. Chlorhexidine in the form of a 4% shampoo and spray used twice weekly was shown to be equally effective as systemic antimicrobial therapy in 31 dogs with superficial pyoderma over a 4‐week period.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0089" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>89</sup>
</xref> Individual studies have compared different chlorhexidine formulations (surgical scrub, shampoo, solution), concentrations and/or administration frequency, finding little statistical difference between groups.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0097" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>97</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0098" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>98</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0099" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>99</sup>
</xref> However, in vitro evidence suggests that low‐concentration chlorhexidine products may only have low efficacy against ESBL‐producing <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> and no efficacy against <italic toggle="yes">Pseudomonas</italic> spp.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0146" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>146</sup>
</xref> This should be considered if it is likely or known that these organisms are present or if rods predominate in cytology findings. Good‐quality evidence for efficacy also was found for a chlorhexidine/miconazole combination, benzoyl peroxide and olanexidine at least for one type of pyoderma (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0012" ref-type="table">12</xref>).</p><p>Support for the use of a dilute sodium hypochlorite (NaOCl; bleach)/salicylic acid combination product (concentrations unknown) was published in 17 dogs with staphylococcal pyoderma where bacterial counts and clinical scores improved with three‐times weekly washes over 4 weeks.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0093" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>93</sup>
</xref> Household bleach is widely available as a domestic disinfectant in concentrations of 3%–8% (strength at time of manufacturing) or of 1.5% in disinfection wipes and sprays for use on household surfaces (not safe to use on skin in these concentrations!). It also is used at much lower concentrations of 0.05–0.005% in baths for humans with atopic eczema.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0147" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>147</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0148" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>148</sup>
</xref> Although chlorine is an excellent bactericidal agent, its bactericidal effect in vivo remains questionable as contact with organic matter on skin has a degrading effect and may reduce activity. Household bleach requires significant dilution to achieve a concentration considered safe for use on dogs (&lt;0.05%),<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0149" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>149</sup>
</xref> creating a potential for mixing errors that could pose a risk to the dog or owner. Another chlorine‐related oxidising agent is hypochlorous acid (ClOH or HClO), which has been shown to be safe as an ear flush solution in dogs at a concentration of 0.015%.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0150" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>150</sup>
</xref> Hypochlorous acid is present within leucocytes of mammals as an endogenous antimicrobial; it is safe for use in open wounds and therefore suitable for the management of pyoderma lesions associated with erosions or ulcers.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0072" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>72</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Other molecules used as antiseptics on skin include acids (e.g. acetic, boric, lactic and salicylic), ethyl lactate, 80% ethyl alcohol, povidone iodine, triclosan, some botanical extracts and metal solutions (e.g. aluminium triacetate solution, Burow's solution, magnesium sulfate, Epsom salt) and chloroxylenol (PCMX; used in household cleaning products and some over‐the‐counter dog shampoos). The antibacterial efficacy of these agents has been suggested by a combination of in vitro work, surgical scrub models, commercial household cleaning data, small clinical studies (not published according to our literature search criteria) and anecdote; however, owing to a lack of clinical efficacy and safety data, these agents cannot be recommended at present for clinical use in canine pyoderma while alternative products with proven efficacy and safety are available (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0012" ref-type="table">12</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0057"><title>Topical antimicrobials or antibiotics</title><p>Some systemically administered antimicrobials (e.g. fusidic acid) also are available as topical formulations, while others are only available for topical therapy (e.g. mupirocin) (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0012" ref-type="table">12</xref>). Although laboratory breakpoints for these agents exist, including for isolates from animals, susceptibility test results reported by laboratories are only meaningful for systemic therapy and do not predict efficacy by the topical route.</p><p>Fusidic acid and mupirocin are known for their narrow‐spectrum anti‐staphylococcal activity. Although there are currently no clinical data on fusidic acid when used alone in canine pyoderma, its efficacy when used as a topical combination product with betamethasone was shown to be equal to that of systemic amoxicillin/clavulanic acid and dexamethasone in dogs with pyotraumatic dermatitis.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0025" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>25</sup>
</xref> In addition, an ex vivo study showed that fusidic acid, applied as a 10‐mg/g ophthalmic suspension, can penetrate to the level of the hair follicle infundibulum at concentrations that greatly exceed typical MICs for canine <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic>.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0151" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>151</sup>
</xref> In one abstract presentation, clinical efficacy of a 0.2% mupirocin spray was suggested to be comparable to a 4% chlorhexidine mousse, when both were applied twice daily for 3 weeks, in reducing lesion and pruritus scores in five dogs with pyoderma (depth not stated).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0152" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>152</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>For other topical antibiotics such as bacitracin, florfenicol, neomycin, nisin, polymyxin B and silver sulfadiazine, weak evidence in canine pyoderma has been published for some (Table <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0001" ref-type="supplementary-material">S1</xref>). Their use may be preferable over systemic antimicrobials in surface and superficial pyoderma where agents listed in Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0012" ref-type="table">12</xref> are not suitable, yet they cannot be recommended with support from published clinical evidence and there is currently no evidence suggesting a benefit over topical chlorhexidine. For some localised, inflamed lesions, for example in surface pyodermas, topical antibiotics may be advocated and effective in the form of multi‐pharma ear drop formulations (off‐licence).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0070" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>70</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0058"><title>Adverse reactions and resistance concerns</title><p>Topical antimicrobial formulations can be considered safe if used according to manufacturers' or literature‐recommended instructions and provided no known hypersensitivities are reported. Nevertheless, adverse reactions such as erythema and pruritus can be seen, usually immediately after application. They tend to be transient and do not require specific treatment. Contact dermatitis can rarely occur after repeated application. In cases where reactions are problematic, changing the formulation and active ingredient or reducing the concentration may be advisable. Chlorhexidine solutions are toxic to the cornea and use around the eyes, including where there is potential for splashes, should be avoided.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0153" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>153</sup>
</xref> For treatment of periocular skin lesions, antimicrobial ophthalmic preparations may be safer. The potential for cutaneous atrophy when using topical glucocorticoid combinations over long terms needs to be considered.</p><p>Resistance or nonsusceptibility to topically used agents has not been convincingly documented or defined to date. Although genes associated with higher MICs to some topical agents have been identified in bacterial pathogens, their presence has not been linked to clinical treatment failure. MICs of staphylococci from dogs have been shown to be low against fusidic acid and chlorhexidine.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0154" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>154</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0155" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>155</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0156" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>156</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Although there have been intermittent reports of in vitro chlorhexidine tolerance in staphylococci, the concentrations of chlorhexidine achieved through appropriate clinical use far exceed the maximum MICs described in the literature, despite decades of use of this active ingredient. No true antimicrobial resistance resulting in clinical treatment failure of staphylococcal infection using chlorhexidine has yet been described.</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0059"><title>Product formulation</title><p>In order to maximise chances of active ingredients reaching the site of infection for the required duration, at the desired concentration, and at the necessary frequency, a practical product formulation needs to be chosen (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0013" ref-type="table">13</xref>). Shampoos are well suited for widespread disease, while nonrinse preparations may be preferable as they are less time‐consuming. Mousses, wipes or sprays may be favoured by some for their suitability for skin fold disease. In addition to their immediate antibacterial effect, some shampoo, spray and mousse/foam formulations provide residual antimicrobial activity on hairs for ≤10 days.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0157" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>157</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0158" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>158</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0159" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>159</sup>
</xref>
</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0013" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 13</label><caption><p>Product formulations available for topical antimicrobial therapy in canine pyoderma.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" <p>May be suitable for painful areas</p>
<p>Easy and clean to apply</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Once or twice daily</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Wipes</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Skin folds, localised lesions</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Easy to use in folds, practical and effective for less‐haired skin</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Once or twice daily</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Soaks</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Widespread lesions, pododermatitis</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1"/><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Daily</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Rinsing needed</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Shampoo</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Widespread lesions in hairy areas (clipping of long coats will aid application and penetration)</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Considerable time and effort required</p>
<p>May interfere with the efficacy of other topically applied products—consider changing to oral ectoparasiticides</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Typically at least two to three times per week</p>
<p>Contact time of 10–15 min recommended</p>
</td></tr></tbody></table></table-wrap></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0060"><title>Practical tips to promote good outcomes</title><p>Many owners will be familiar with the concept of topical therapies as they are widely used in human medicine for the management of skin conditions. However, the application of topical therapy to hairy, sometimes uncooperative, dogs several times daily can be difficult and often more challenging than systemic treatment. The risk of compliance failure can be minimised by highlighting some of the advantages of topical therapy to enhance acceptance or ‘buy‐in’ for topical treatments and treatment success (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0008" ref-type="boxed-text">8</xref>).</p><p>Strategies to improve the success of topical therapy can include the following:
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0020"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0156"><p>Clipping of long, matted or thick hair coats can make application easier.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0157"><p>Removing excess debris using water and wipes before the application of topical products, as active ingredients may be inactivated by organic material including biofilm.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0158"><p>Ensuring contact of product with lesional skin</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0159"><p>Allowing adequate contact time for shampoo formulations; a 10‐ to 15‐min contact time before rinsing is currently recommended.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0160"><p>Tailoring of product formulations to dog/lesion and owner needs (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0012" ref-type="table">13</xref>)</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0161"><p>Combining different product formulations (same or similar active ingredient) may improve acceptance; for example, replacing shampoos on some days with a mousse/foam, spray or solution that do not require wetting and rinsing off.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0162"><p>Distracting dogs from removing active ingredients through play, walks or feeding immediately after treatment</p></list-item></list>
</p><p>Careful instructions on shampooing and soaking, application of sufficient product, concentration, contact time and rinsing may be needed. One study determined the amount of 2% chlorhexidine acetate shampoo needed for bathing a two‐hand‐sized area of infected skin to be the size of a 26.5‐mm diameter coin when dispensed.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0099" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>99</sup>
</xref> Manufacturers of topical products are encouraged to provide specific dosing recommendations (e.g. pumps per kg body weight or m<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0002" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>2</sup>
</xref>) to ensure maximum efficacy.</p><p>Some topical therapy combinations may be offered by compounding pharmacies. These are typically compounded (formulated) using bulk chemical substances and other ingredients. Compounded products are not approved by regulatory authorities and the quality, strength, safety and effectiveness cannot be assured. Some regulatory authorities prohibit compounding from bulk chemical substances (e.g. <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/cvm-gfi-256-compounding-animal-drugs-bulk-drug-substances" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.fda.gov/regulatory‐information/search‐fda‐guidance‐documents/cvm‐gfi‐256‐compounding‐animal‐drugs‐bulk‐drug‐substances</ext-link>).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0160" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>160</sup>
</xref> Whenever possible, use approved products.</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0061"><label>9</label><title>SYSTEMIC ANTIMICROBIAL THERAPY FOR CANINE PYODERMA</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0062"><title>General comments</title><p>Systemic antimicrobials should be reserved for deep pyoderma or for a small number of cases of superficial pyoderma where topical therapy alone has not been effective or is not feasible (Figure <xref rid="vde13342-fig-0001" ref-type="fig">1</xref>).</p><p>For this version of the canine pyoderma guidelines, we assigned antimicrobial drugs to four groups in order of selection priority: first‐choice, second‐choice, reserved and strongly discouraged (Tables <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0014" ref-type="table">14</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0015" ref-type="table">15</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0016" ref-type="table">16</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0017" ref-type="table">17</xref>). Groupings reflect the authors' considerations of efficacy and safety based on published evidence and the importance of a drug in human medicine. For our document, drug allocations were created following an iterative process and unanimously agreed upon by our author panel except for the potentiated sulfonamides. This drug class was moved from the first‐choice group into the second‐choice group by majority vote, not for resistance concerns but owing to concerns over clinical adverse events for dogs. Another drug assigned to a different group compared to the earlier ISCAID guidelines for canine bacterial folliculitis<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref> is cefovecin, which has been placed into the second‐choice group by unanimous vote. This change is in line with datasheet recommendations in most countries. These state that the use of third‐generation cephalosporins should be based on susceptibility testing.</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0014" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 14</label><caption><p>‘First‐choice drugs’ for canine pyoderma if systemic therapy is indicated.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead><tr><th align="left" colspan="4" style="background-color:#C5E0B3" rowspan="1">First‐choice drugs: good, predicted efficacy against most meticillin‐susceptible <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus</italic> spp., low risk of adverse effects</th></tr><tr><th align="left" style="background-color:#C5E0B3" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Class</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#C5E0B3" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Drug &amp; suggested dose</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#C5E0B3" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comments &amp; evidence base in canine pyoderma</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#C5E0B3" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Resistance considerations</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">β‐lactams</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">

</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0028"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0177"><p>No approved susceptibility testing standards and breakpoints available for veterinary isolates</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0007"><fn id="vde13342-note-0017"><p>Abbreviations: p.o.: per os (administered orally); q12h: twice daily every 12 h.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>We steered away from the terms ‘tier’, ‘line’ and ‘category’ to avoid confusion with other classification systems. These systems are used to reflect the likelihood of an agent to produce resistant bacteria that can be a public health risk and help to prioritise low risk drugs over those of critical importance for human health. Unfortunately, a degree of confusion has developed with the wider use of ranking systems. For example, the ‘first tier’ classification by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA)<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0161" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>161</sup>
</xref> relates to the most critical and important drugs for human health whereas the ‘first tier’ or ‘first line’ category in earlier veterinary pyoderma guidelines<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0162" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>162</sup>
</xref> comprises the ‘preferred use’ agents.</p><p>Drugs authorised for use in canine pyoderma should be used whenever possible. Authorisation processes will have involved efficacy and safety testing, which will improve chances of successful outcomes. Recommended dosages are expected to achieve therapeutic drug concentrations against <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> in well‐perfused canine skin. The recommended doses are in agreement with those listed in Appendix D of the CLSI veterinary standards as well as pharmacokinetic‐pharmacodynamic (PK‐PD) assessment.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0048" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>48</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>General concepts of antimicrobial prescribing, such as frequent weighing of the dog, accurate dosing and reminding owners of the need for good compliance, are important. Maintaining regular administration intervals is particularly important for time‐dependent antimicrobials such as the β‐lactams, while doses at the high end of the dosing range are recommended for concentration‐dependent drugs such as the fluoroquinolones.</p><p>Drugs in this document are spelt according to the International Nonproprietary Name (INN) (e.g. cefalexin, rifampicin) which may differ from other drug nomenclature such as the United States Adopted Names (USAN) (cephalexin, rifampin).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0063"><title>Culture‐based treatment</title><p>For MSSP, first‐choice drugs should be used unless clinical considerations (not simply convenience) make agents from this group unsuitable (e.g. known drug hypersensitivities, a clinical or safety need for longer dosing intervals). If other drug options are needed, second‐choice drugs, including the potentiated sulfonamides (formerly listed as first‐choice drugs for pyoderma), can be considered provided that potential clinical adverse effects and an increased risk of selecting for drug resistance (e.g. fluoroquinolones)<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0163" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>163</sup>
</xref> are taken into account.</p><p>If MRSP is isolated, first‐ or second‐choice drugs may still be appropriate if in vitro susceptibility is indicated and treatment with those agents can translate into clinical efficacy (see also Section ‘Meticillin‐resistant staphylococcal pyoderma’). However, for multidrug‐resistant MRSP, agents from the reserved drugs group might be needed.</p><p>If BC/AST yields mixed bacterial growth with varied susceptibility patterns, its individual relevance should be assessed (see Section ‘Bacterial culture and antimicrobial susceptibility testing (BC/AST)’).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0064"><title>Empirical choices</title><p>Empirical systemic antimicrobial therapy should only be considered where the risk for drug resistance is low.</p><p>Only first‐choice drugs (clindamycin, cefalexin, cefadroxil, amoxicillin‐clavulanate) should be considered for empirical therapy.</p><p>First‐choice drugs are expected to have good efficacy against MSSP (SOR A).</p><p>Drug choices based on BC/AST results will always be preferable, yet empirical choices for pyoderma treatment may be appropriate if cytology findings support a staphylococcal infection and if there is a low suspicion for MRS based on clinical, historical and geographical information. This applies to some cases of uncomplicated infections that are likely to resolve quickly, those that have not received systemic antimicrobial therapy previously for any reason (or very little) and to dogs that live in areas with a low prevalence of MRS.</p><p>If first‐choice drugs are unsuitable (e.g. known drug hypersensitivities, clinical or safety reasons requiring longer dosing intervals), BC/AST should inform the selection of second‐choice or other group drugs.</p><p>Because cost (and occasionally practicality) is likely to be the main driver for empirical selection of drugs, owners should be made aware that the cost of antimicrobial resistance may ultimately exceed that of BC/AST, should empirically chosen drugs turn out to be ineffective; likewise, practices and laboratories should support antimicrobial stewardship by charging reasonably for BC/AST.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0164" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>164</sup>
</xref> Resistance to some first‐choice agents among MSSP is higher in dogs that have previously received systemic antimicrobial therapy and in those presenting to referral centres.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0165" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>165</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0166" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>166</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0167" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>167</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0168" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>168</sup>
</xref> The economic and clinical value of BC/AST also increases with the prevalence of meticillin‐resistance among <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> clinical isolates and in situations where the risk of MRSP involvement is high. Testing is considered critical in certain situations (Box <xref rid="vde13342-fea-0002" ref-type="boxed-text">2</xref>), in dogs presenting to referral centres and those with a history of prolonged or repeated antimicrobial treatment, MRS infection, or with MRS infection in their household.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0065"><title>First‐choice drugs</title><p>Drugs listed as ‘first‐choice drugs’ (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0014" ref-type="table">14</xref>) are expected to show good efficacy in dogs with pyoderma owing to meticillin‐susceptible staphylococci on the basis of approval by regulatory authorities (e.g. European Medicines Agency (EMA), FDA) and based on published clinical trial evidence.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0008" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>8</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Representatives of first‐choice drugs are expected to be available and approved in most countries for the treatment of canine skin infections and susceptibility testing breakpoints for staphylococci from dogs are available for all except lincomycin.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0048" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>48</sup>
</xref> Dosages listed here may deviate from previous recommendations for staphylococcal skin infections in dogs and from registration datasheets. They are based on a careful review of the current approved label recommendations in various countries, updated PK‐PD analysis, and the dose that was used to determine approved susceptibility testing breakpoints (Appendix D of CLSI 2024 standards).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0048" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>48</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0066"><title>Second‐choice drugs</title><p>Drugs listed as ‘second‐choice’ should <italic toggle="yes">only</italic> be considered (i) when the causative bacterium is susceptible based on BC/AST results, <italic toggle="yes">and</italic> (ii) when first‐choice agents are not appropriate (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0015" ref-type="table">15</xref>). They are ranked as ‘second‐choice’ either because of an increased relative risk for the selection of important multidrug‐resistant pathogens in pets and humans<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0161" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>161</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0176" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>176</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0177" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>177</sup>
</xref> or as a consequence of an increased risk of adverse events.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0178" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>178</sup>
</xref> Compared to first‐choice drugs, approval status, regulatory legislation and availability of these agents are more variable across the world.</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0015" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 15</label><caption><p>‘Second‐choice drugs’ for canine pyoderma if systemic therapy is indicated.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" colspan="4" style="background-color:#FFFF00" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Second‐choice drugs: only to be considered when BC/AST results are available, and when first‐choice drugs are not suitable</th></tr><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" style="background-color:#FFFF00" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Class</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#FFFF00" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Drug &amp; suggested dose</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#FFFF00" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comments &amp; evidence base in canine pyoderma</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#FFFF00" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Resistance considerations</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">Third‐generation cephalosporins</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">

</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">

</td><td align="left" rowspan="2" valign="top" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0044"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0216"><p>Human/veterinary potentiated sulfonamides</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0217"><p>Dogs are more susceptible to adverse effects than other animals</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0218"><p>May be considered for dogs in otherwise good general health and if owners have been made aware of a greater risk for clinically significant adverse effects.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0219"><p>Dose‐dependent adverse effects: keratoconjunctivitis sicca (occurs in about 15% of dogs, reversible when treatment is promptly stopped), folate deficiency, drug‐induced hypothyroidism</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0220"><p>Idiosyncratic adverse reactions/sulfonamide hypersensitivity: Overall risk greater than with other antimicrobials and true incidence unclear (0.25%–10% depending on study design<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0193" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>193</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0194" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>194</sup>
</xref>; fever, blood dyscrasias, polyarthropathy, skin eruptions, acute hepatopathy (risk may be very low for ormetoprim‐sulfadimethoxine owing to a different elimination pathway in dogs), proteinuria</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0221"><p>A breed‐associated increased risk may exist for Dobermans yet evidence for this remains unclear</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0222"><p>Avoid in dogs with a known drug‐hypersensitivity</p></list-item></list>

</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Ormetoprim‐sulfadimethoxine</p>
<p>55 mg/kg p.o. loading dose, then 27.5 mg/kg p.o. q24h</p>
</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0008"><fn id="vde13342-note-0018"><p>Abbreviations: CLSI, Clinical Laboratory Standards Institute; EUCAST, European Committee on Antimicrobial susceptibility testing; q12h, twice daily every 12 h; q24h, once daily every 24 h.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>Changes to this drug group, compared to the previous pyoderma guidelines,<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0005" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>5</sup>
</xref> are based on updates for laboratory testing standards (e.g. higher recommended doses for second‐generation fluoroquinolones),<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0179" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>179</sup>
</xref> new data on the impact on the wider microbiome and extended discussions among the authors about clinical considerations. Although the potentiated sulfonamides may be effective against staphylococci and only have a minor impact on further drug resistance, the risk of adverse events in dogs is well documented. Their selection should be based on BC/AST results if available safer options have been excluded. Also, we no longer included ciprofloxacin, a human‐label generic oral fluoroquinolone, among the fluoroquinolones. Although ciprofloxacin has a similar in vitro activity against staphylococci as veterinary fluoroquinolones, in dogs its oral absorption is low and its bioavailability highly variable.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0180" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>180</sup>
</xref> Because approved testing standards and breakpoints for veterinary isolates are not currently available for ciprofloxacin and human breakpoints for this drug are inappropriate for veterinary isolates, meaningful susceptibility testing for ciprofloxacin is not possible and effective doses for dogs have not been established. The tetracyclines have been moved to the second‐choice group owing to a ≤ 50% chance of resistance when selected for empirical treatment.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0067"><title>Reserved antimicrobial drugs</title><p>Drugs listed as ‘reserved’ should be limited to treating infections caused by multidrug‐resistant staphylococci, mainly MRSP, when no other first‐ or second‐choice options are appropriate (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0016" ref-type="table">16</xref>).</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0016" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 16</label><caption><p>Reserved antimicrobials drugs that may be considered for dogs with pyoderma owing to multidrug‐resistant, meticillin‐resistant staphylococcal pathogens (MRSP, MRSA, MRSC).</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead><tr><th align="left" colspan="4" style="background-color:#F8A808" rowspan="1">Reserved antimicrobial drugs: to be considered <italic toggle="yes">only</italic> when BC/AST results are available, for the management of multidrug‐resistant, meticillin‐resistant staphylococcal pathogens when first‐ and second‐choice drugs are not suitable, and all limitations have been discussed with owners</th></tr><tr><th align="left" style="background-color:#F8A808" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Class</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#F8A808" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Drug &amp; suggested dose</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#F8A808" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comments &amp; evidence base</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#F8A808" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Resistance considerations</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ansamycins</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
