<p>Chloramphenicol</p>
<p>40–50 mg/kg p.o. q8h (consider the lower range of the dose for large dogs)</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0050"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0246"><p>Many adverse effects are possible in animals</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0247"><p>Important: pet owners must be aware of the risks to their own health from accidental exposure: gastrointestinal, neurological, myelosuppression, particularly with longer use</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0248"><p>Owing to the risk (rare) of idiosyncratic, aplastic anaemia in humans, avoid direct contact, wear gloves</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0249"><p>Consider recheck every 2 weeks</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0250"><p>Approved for use in dogs in some countries, yet human‐label or restricted in others; consult local antimicrobial policies and restrictions</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0251"><p>Potent inhibitor of major cytochrome P450 enzymes<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0200" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>200</sup>
</xref>
</p></list-item></list>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Most <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus</italic> spp., including MRSP, will be resistant using the new CLSI testing standards and breakpoints<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0048" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>48</sup>
</xref>
</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0009"><fn id="vde13342-note-0019"><p>Abbreviations: CLSI, Clinical Laboratory Standards Institute; MRSP, meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus pseudintermedius</italic>; MRSA, meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic>; MRSC, meticillin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">S. coagulans</italic> (formerly: <italic toggle="yes">S. schleiferi)</italic>; q8h, every 8 hours; q12h, twice daily every 12 h; q24 h, once daily every 24 h.</p></fn><fn id="vde13342-note-0020"><label>
<sup>a</sup>
</label><p>Rifampicin is the International Nonproprietary Name (INN) and the British Approved Name (BAN); rifampin is the US Adapted Name (USAN); rifamycin is an older name for the same drug, which is no longer used.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>They are either not authorised for or in some countries banned from use in dogs because they are critically important for the treatment of serious infections in human medicine such as tuberculosis. Carbapenems are not included in our recommendations as they are not expected to be effective in MRSP infections and their use is considered inappropriate in canine pyoderma. Furthermore, all drugs in this group are associated with a risk of dose‐dependent predictable and/or idiosyncratic unpredictable serious adverse effects in dogs. Experience with and data on the use of these drugs and their clinical adverse effects are limited.</p><p>Microbiology laboratories may not routinely report susceptibilities for these agents. Consequently, extended susceptibility testing may have to be requested specifically for individual isolates. Given the importance of reserved antimicrobials for human medicine and to limit increasing drug resistance in the patient, consideration of the following five criteria is strongly recommended when evaluating the selection of reserved group antimicrobials.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0195" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>195</sup>
</xref>
<list list-type="order" id="vde13342-list-0055"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0262"><p>BC/AST results from a recent, representative sample indicate that the infecting MRS is susceptible to the agent under consideration and no other treatment options are appropriate.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0263"><p>The risks for adverse treatment effects have been evaluated after thorough clinical assessment of the dog's health status.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0264"><p>The prognosis for resolving the pyoderma is good and it is likely that resolution can be achieved within an acceptable duration of treatment.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0265"><p>Underlying primary causes have been identified, are treatable, and a plan is in place for long‐term management and prevention of recurrent secondary bacterial infections.</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0266"><p>Owners have been made aware of the risks associated with treatment, of the need for compliance, and they are committed to the potentially life‐long follow‐up care needed.</p></list-item></list>
</p><p>If not <italic toggle="yes">all</italic> of these criteria are met, the potential harm from using reserved drugs for dog and human health will likely outweigh the short‐term benefit of a temporary resolution of infection, and other management options should be sought (see Section ‘What if recurrences keep happening?’).</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0068"><title>Strongly discouraged antimicrobial drugs</title><p>Drugs listed as ‘strongly discouraged’ are not licensed for use in animals (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0017" ref-type="table">17</xref>). They are of critical importance in human medicine for the treatment of serious infections involving multidrug‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">Enterococcus</italic> spp. Their use in all animals has been banned in the European Union since 2023.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0201" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>201</sup>
</xref> Although these drugs typically show good in vitro activity against veterinary isolates of <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus</italic> spp., their use in canine pyoderma is strongly discouraged. First, there is a risk of resistance emerging from treatment in the targeted pathogen but also in the commensal microbiota of the dog, with the potential for zoonotic transmission to in‐contact people. Secondly, veterinarians would rarely have sufficient experience with the use of these drugs to ensure safe and effective prescribing. If considering drugs from this group, we encourage consultation with or referral to a clinical expert (specialist) in a relevant area (infectious diseases, clinical pharmacology, dermatology) before prescribing.</p><table-wrap position="float" id="vde13342-tbl-0017" content-type="TABLE" orientation="portrait"><label>TABLE 17</label><caption><p>Antimicrobials for which the use in canine pyoderma is strongly discouraged.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><col align="left" span="1"/><thead valign="bottom"><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" colspan="4" style="background-color:#FF0000" valign="bottom" rowspan="1">Strongly discouraged antimicrobial drugs</th></tr><tr style="border-bottom:solid 1px #000000"><th align="left" style="background-color:#FF0000" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Class</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#FF0000" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Drug</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#FF0000" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comments &amp; evidence base</th><th align="left" style="background-color:#FF0000" valign="bottom" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Resistance consideration</th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Oxazolidinone</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Linezolid</p>
<p>10 mg/kg p.o. q12h</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0051"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0252"><p>In people, administration for &gt;2 weeks, without careful monitoring, is discouraged because of reversible myelosuppression. Myelosuppression has been described in adult dogs used in drug‐safety testing<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0202" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>202</sup>
</xref>
</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0253"><p>Restricted for use in dogs by regulatory authorities in some countries; consult local antimicrobial policies and restrictions</p></list-item></list>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0052"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0254"><p>No approved susceptibility testing breakpoints available for isolates from animals; when testing canine isolates, human breakpoints and testing standards may be applied</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0255"><p>Linezolid‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus</italic> spp. associated with ribosomal mutations are rare, yet plasmid‐mediated resistance mediated by the <italic toggle="yes">cfr</italic> gene can occur in isolates from animals</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr><tr><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Glycopeptide</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<p>Vancomycin</p>
<p>15 mg/kg q6‐8h i.v. slow infusion (frequency of administration based on clinical monitoring)</p>
<p>Constant‐rate infusion (CRI): loading dose of 3.5 mg/kg, followed by CRI of 1.5 mg/kg mixed in 5% dextrose in water</p>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0053"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0256"><p>Must be administered to dogs by slow i.v. infusion. Oral vancomycin cannot be used as it is not absorbed by dogs</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0257"><p>Monitoring blood concentrations of vancomycin, preferably using the AUC (area under the curve), is recommended to optimise treatment and to avoid adverse effects and treatment failure.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0203" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>203</sup>
</xref> This type of monitoring is rarely available to veterinarians</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0258"><p>Risk of nephrotoxicity in dogs</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0259"><p>Restricted for use in animals by regulatory authorities in some countries; consult local antimicrobial policies and restrictions</p></list-item></list>
</td><td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<list list-type="bullet" id="vde13342-list-0054"><list-item id="vde13342-li-0260"><p>No approved susceptibility testing breakpoints available for isolates from animals; when testing canine isolates, human breakpoints and testing standards may be applied</p></list-item><list-item id="vde13342-li-0261"><p>Although there are no vancomycin‐resistant staphylococci reported from dogs to date, vancomycin‐resistant <italic toggle="yes">Saureus</italic> (VRSA), is a serious health concern in human medicine worldwide<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0204" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>204</sup>
</xref>
</p></list-item></list>
</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot id="vde13342-ntgp-0010"><fn id="vde13342-note-0021"><p>Abbreviations: q12h, twice daily; q6–8h, three or four times a day.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0069"><label>10</label><title>PREVENTING RECURRENCES OF PYODERMA</title><p>If underlying primary causes remain undiagnosed or untreated, all types of pyoderma can recur even following successful antimicrobial treatment.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0205" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>205</sup>
</xref> However, managing recurrences with repeated courses of systemic antimicrobials and historical strategies such as pulse‐ or low‐dose antimicrobial therapy is no longer considered safe or appropriate and must be avoided.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0206" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>206</sup>
</xref>
</p><sec id="vde13342-sec-0070"><title>Undiagnosed underlying causes</title><p>In dogs that require systemic antimicrobial therapy more than once a year for recurrent pyoderma, the search for underlying primary causes must be intensified or repeated.</p><p>Based on findings from molecular studies, staphylococci isolated from pyoderma lesions are genetically indistinguishable from those at carriage sites.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0041" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>41</sup>
</xref> Thus, host factors such as altered immune, physical or chemical skin defence mechanisms must be present for pyoderma to occur, even if such factors cannot be easily diagnosed with available diagnostic tests. The concept of ‘idiopathic’ or ‘primary’ pyoderma is no longer tenable.</p><p>A thorough diagnostic review of potential primary underlying diseases should be repeated at least every 6–12 months, with the choice of tests guided by the skin lesions and clinical signs that remain after pyoderma has been resolved through successful treatment (see Section ‘Diagnostic approach and diagnostic tests’). Briefly, where the age of onset of recurrent pyoderma is young (&lt;3 years), allergic skin disease is one of the most likely underlying causes. In older dogs, blood and urine analyses, including testing for endocrinopathies, imaging for neoplastic conditions, or skin histopathological evaluation, should be considered over time as some underlying conditions may only become detectable in more advanced stages. Ectoparasite prophylaxis, appropriate for regionally prevalent ectoparasites, should be in place before antimicrobial therapy is considered.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0071"><title>Recurrent pyoderma in atopic dogs</title><p>In dogs with recurrent superficial pyoderma secondary to underlying allergic skin disease, appropriate medication or management strategies for their allergy should be prioritised over repeated treatment with antimicrobials.</p><p>Allergic, including atopic, skin diseases are suspected and diagnosed clinically if erythema and pruritus remain at predilection sites after ectoparasites and microbial infections have been resolved.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0207" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>207</sup>
</xref> The most appropriate drugs and management options for controlling allergic skin disease depend on the clinical signs and stage of disease in each case.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0014" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>14</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0208" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>208</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0209" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>209</sup>
</xref> If inflammatory signs predominate, especially if chronic‐hyperplastic changes are found, glucocorticoids and ciclosporin may be most suitable to control disease. In cases where pruritus is pronounced but inflammation is minimal, alternative anti‐pruritic drugs such as Janus kinase inhibitors (e.g. oclacitinib) or interleukin‐31 monoclonal antibodies (e.g. lokivetmab) also be adequate.</p><p>It is generally recommended to resolve a microbial infection first before starting immune‐modulatory treatment. This recommendation applies to all deep pyodermas where the risk of systemic spread may be high. In superficial and surface pyoderma, resolving the pyoderma first, before starting anti‐inflammatory medication, will allow the lowest necessary dose of the chosen anti‐allergy medication to be established. However, where dogs are already receiving treatment for their primary allergic disease or where signs of allergic disease are severe, starting, continuing or upgrading anti‐inflammatory treatment early may be of low risk for general health (provided doses are not immunosuppressive and progress can be monitored) and may actually be helpful by normalising the local skin microenvironment and thus improving resilience against infection.</p><p>Although seemingly counterintuitive, glucocorticoids (systemically or topically) or ciclosporin or other drugs to manage allergic pruritus (oclacitinib, lokivetmab) are best used to improve control of allergic skin disease and proactively prevent secondary bacterial infections. Evidence specific for which drugs to use to prevent recurrence of pyoderma in allergic dogs is lacking, yet studies suggest that prednisolone and oclacitinib may reduce the need for antimicrobial drugs in some atopic dogs.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0210" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>210</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0211" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>211</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0072"><title>What if recurrences keep happening?</title><p>Proactive topical antimicrobial therapy with antiseptics may be effective in preventing relapses and can be maintained indefinitely.</p><p>Where pyoderma recurs despite continuing efforts to find and manage underlying causes, antimicrobial treatment will be required to resolve recurrent lesions and associated pruritus or pain. Topical antiseptics (e.g. 2%–4% chlorhexidine products) applied daily or 2–3 times weekly as needed may be necessary (see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’). Whether this approach also is effective in preventing recurrences of pyoderma has not been proven and future studies are urgently needed.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0073"><title>Current or future alternative treatment options</title><p>Few alternatives to antimicrobial therapy have been investigated or are available.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0212" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>212</sup>
</xref> Autogenous <italic toggle="yes">S. pseudintermedius</italic> bacterins and S. <italic toggle="yes">aureus</italic> lysate were well‐tolerated and have been shown to reduce the need for antimicrobial treatment in dogs with recurrent pyoderma in several small studies.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0213" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>213</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0214" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>214</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0215" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>215</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0216" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>216</sup>
</xref> Unfortunately, their availability is currently very limited in many countries due to licensing difficulties.</p><p>While bacteriophage therapy, skin probiotics and bacterial interference approaches may become available in the future, clinical studies in dogs with pyoderma have not been published to date.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0217" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>217</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0218" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>218</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0219" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>219</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0074"><label>11</label><title>METICILLIN‐RESISTANT STAPHYLOCOCCAL PYODERMA</title><sec id="vde13342-sec-0075"><title>General comments</title><p>General management concepts and treatment recommendations for surface, superficial, and deep pyoderma apply also to MRS pyoderma and additional detail can be found in the MRS‐specific guidelines, available from the World Association for Veterinary Dermatology (WAVD).<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Briefly, meticillin‐resistance in staphylococci is used as a marker for resistance to virtually all β‐lactam antibiotics, including first‐ and third‐generation cephalosporins and carbapenems. While there is no direct relationship between meticillin‐resistance and resistance to other antimicrobial classes, MRS have often acquired multiple additional resistances and many are resistant to most veterinary antimicrobials. This can make treatment selection for systemic therapy challenging, yet topically used agents, both antiseptics and topical antibiotics, have remained effective (Table <xref rid="vde13342-supitem-0001" ref-type="supplementary-material">S1</xref>). The main risk factors for MRSP infections in dogs include repeated courses of systemic antimicrobials, chronic disease associated with repeated visits to veterinary premises, hospitalisation and some surgical procedures.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0220" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>220</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0221" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>221</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0222" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>222</sup>
</xref> Pyoderma associated with MRS is clinically indistinguishable from pyoderma involving meticillin‐susceptible staphylococci and BC/AST is required for identification.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0222" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>222</sup>
</xref> The prognosis for MRS pyoderma is considered good, although one study has reported a longer duration to resolution of infection, which is likely to be associated with chronicity of infections.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0091" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>91</sup>
</xref> Because of the ability of multidrug‐resistant staphylococci to spread among dogs, between different host species, and to contaminate their environments, infection control measures and the potential for zoonotic transmission require consideration.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>If meticillin resistance is reported in coagulase‐negative staphylococci from skin, their clinical relevance may be doubtful and careful consideration of clinical signs and cytology is recommended before antimicrobial therapy is prescribed.</p><p>Coagulase‐negative staphylococci are widely distributed as commensals in animals and humans and are typically considered as nonpathogenic or barely pathogenic or as contaminants when reported from skin samples by BC/AST. Multidrug resistance, including meticillin resistance, is common in coagulase‐negative staphylococci yet will not trigger the same concerns for treatment and infection control measures as in coagulase‐positive staphylococci, except in immunocompromised individuals or where infection is associated with implants.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0223" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>223</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0224" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>224</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>The relevance of coagulase‐negative staphylococci as pathogens in a skin infection needs to be confirmed through review of the clinical history, physical examination, cytology and review of sampling and culture method. A coagulase‐negative <italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus</italic> isolated from intact primary lesions (e.g. a pustule or a furuncle ruptured in a sterile manner) or grown in pure culture from a tissue sample obtained through sterile biopsy technique is more likely to be a true pathogen than if isolated from a surface swab (Section ‘Diagnostic approach and diagnostic tests’). Accurate speciation of staphylococci by veterinary microbiology laboratories is therefore important.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0223" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>223</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0224" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>224</sup>
</xref>
</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0076"><title>Treatment of <styled-content style="fixed-case" toggle="no">MRS</styled-content> infection</title><p>Topical antimicrobial therapy as the sole antibacterial treatment modality is the treatment‐of‐choice for all cases of surface and superficial MRS pyoderma (SOR A).</p><p>If systemic therapy is needed for MRS pyoderma and if susceptibility is reported for non‐beta lactam, first‐choice or second‐choice antimicrobials, these should be prescribed.</p><p>If reserved‐group drugs (Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0016" ref-type="table">16</xref>) are considered, recent laboratory test results need to be available, the larger clinical context needs to be acknowledged, and owners need to be fully aware of the more complex treatment requirements to avoid inappropriate use and disappointing treatment outcomes (see Section ‘Reserved antimicrobial drugs’).</p><p>Adjunctive topical antimicrobial therapy is recommended for every case of pyoderma that involves meticillin‐resistant staphylococci.</p><p>Antiseptics and topical antibiotics that have proven efficacy against staphylococci are expected to be effective against MRS also (see Section ‘Topical antimicrobial therapy’). Genes associated with higher MICs for chlorhexidine and some topical antibiotics have been identified in MRS from dogs, yet their clinical relevance remains unclear, and clinical treatment failures have not been reported with topical therapy.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0155" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>155</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>For systemic antimicrobial therapy, clindamycin may occasionally be appropriate from the first‐choice drug group if in vitro susceptibility is indicated by the report. Furthermore, dogs infected with ‘low‐level’ MRSP isolates may respond favourably to cefalexin or amoxicillin/clavulanic acid. These MRSP isolates typically display MICs of oxacillin between 0.5 and 2 mg/L, are cefalexin‐susceptible according to CLSI standards and breakpoints.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0225" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>225</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>If second choice‐drugs are needed and if susceptibility to fluoroquinolones is reported, it should be confirmed with the laboratory that the most recently revised CLSI breakpoints were used<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0179" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>179</sup>
</xref> and the higher doses recommended in Table <xref rid="vde13342-tbl-0016" ref-type="table">16</xref> used.</p><p>Within the reserved drugs group, most evidence and information is available for rifampicin in the treatment of MRSP pyoderma.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0226" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>226</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0227" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>227</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0228" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>228</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0229" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>229</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0230" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>230</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0231" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>231</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0232" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>232</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0233" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>233</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0234" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>234</sup>
</xref> Little is published on the use of amikacin for canine pyoderma; however, information on adverse drug effects is available based on experience in the management of other infections, especially those associated with the urinary tract.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0197" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>197</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0198" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>198</sup>
</xref>
</p><p>Use of other drugs such as the oxazolidinones or glycopeptides in dogs is strongly discouraged and may be legally restricted in some countries. Their use can be considered unwarranted in cases of canine pyoderma where alternatives can be identified. Consultation with a dermatology, internal medicine, microbiology or infection control specialist is recommended to identify suitable alternative management plans.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0077"><title>Infection control measures for the veterinary clinic or hospital</title><p>Dogs with MRS infection will contaminate their environment with multidrug‐resistant bacteria. Additionally, the majority (&gt;60%) of dogs that have recovered from MRSP infection continued to carry MRSP on healthy skin and mucosae for many months.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0229" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>229</sup>
</xref> Dogs also will continue to contaminate clinical areas when visiting veterinary premises through natural shedding of hair and skin cells with adherent bacteria and from respiratory secretions. For MRSA, studies have shown that viable MRSA can be recovered from dry surfaces after 6 months and longer,<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0230" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>230</sup>
</xref> and similar long environmental survival is expected for MRSP. Implementation of cleaning and disinfection protocols, including rigorous hand hygiene, should be instituted when a dog with MRS infection is visiting and guidelines for practice infection control measures are widely available, for example from the Ontario Animal Health Network.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0231" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>231</sup>
</xref> However, further measures such as screening and isolation strategies for dogs with MRSP pyoderma have not been evaluated in veterinary settings yet. In human medicine, it has been shown that the effectiveness and cost‐efficiency of screening and isolation measures varied with the local prevalence of MRSA and the intensity of the hospital setting.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0232" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>232</sup>
</xref> Given the wide range of regional MRSP prevalence rates across the world, recommendations on best screening and isolation practices will need to be made regionally, possibly in collaboration with local infection control advisory colleagues.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0078"><title>Zoonotic potential</title><p>The risk of MRS transmission from dogs to humans is considered low for MRSP, potentially higher for the mostly human‐hospital‐associated pathogen MRSA and unknown for MRSC.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0006" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>6</sup>
</xref> Rare human infections with MRSP have been reported, and owners should be informed of the zoonotic potential and of the merits of rigorous hand hygiene and reducing exposure to infectious material.<xref rid="vde13342-bib-0233" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>233</sup>
</xref>, <xref rid="vde13342-bib-0234" ref-type="bibr">
<sup>234</sup>
</xref> However, the presence of MRSA suggests a probable human source of infection. Thus, it is advisable to talk to pet owners about the possibility of human‐to‐animal transmission of MRSA in the household when discussing management. Awareness about potential exposure to MDR bacteria from a dog's infection is particularly important for people with co‐morbidities or other risk factors for infection. For further concerns, owners should be advised to consult their own medical practitioner who can assess the risk of zoonotic transmission in light of the owner's medical history and that of other household members.</p></sec></sec><sec sec-type="conclusions" id="vde13342-sec-0079"><title>CONCLUSIONS</title><p>With the skin being easily accessible for sampling and topical therapy, it offers unique opportunities for antimicrobial stewardship. Routine use of cytology, topical antimicrobial therapy and a focus on correcting underlying primary causes that lead to pyoderma will substantially reduce our reliance on systemic antimicrobials.</p><p>For cases that need systemic antimicrobial therapy, information presented in the document will help clinicians to choose and use antimicrobials responsibly according to available evidence and consensus. Adherence to the guidelines will increase the chances for successful treatment outcomes and reduce the risk of inappropriate selection pressure on bacterial pathogens and commensal flora. Knowledge gaps identified during the guideline process include the need for a better characterisation of some of the clinical presentations of canine pyoderma to improve diagnosis and, importantly, a standardisation of study design and outcome measures for clinical trials.</p></sec><sec id="vde13342-sec-0085"><title>AUTHOR CONTRIBUTIONS</title><p>
